Beyond ReLU: How Activations Affect Neural Kernels and Random Wide Networks
David Holzmüller, Max David Schölpple

Abstract
While the theory of deep learning has made some progress in recent years, much of it is limited to the ReLU activation function. In particular, while the neural tangent kernel (NTK) and neural network Gaussian process kernel (NNGP) have given theoreticians tractable limiting cases of fully connected neural networks, their properties for most activation functions except for powers of the ReLU function are poorly understood. Our main contribution is to provide a more general characterization of the RKHS of these kernels for typical activation functions whose only non-smoothness is at zero, such as SELU, ELU, or LeakyReLU. Our analysis also covers a broad set of special cases such as missing biases, two-layer networks, or polynomial activations. Our results show that a broad class of not infinitely smooth activations generate equivalent RKHSs at different network depths, while polynomial activations generate non-equivalent RKHSs. Finally, we derive results for the smoothness of NNGP sample paths, characterizing the smoothness of infinitely wide neural networks at initialization.



1
Introduction

Despite great efforts, our theoretical understanding of when and why deep learning works remains limited, and much of it is distributed across fragmented case studies that do not integrate into a bigger theory. For example, the influence of activation functions on the training dynamics and generalization behavior of deep learning methods is unclear.

One of the most prominent approaches towards deep learning theory is the study of infinite-width limits of neural networks
(
Neal 1996
;
Jacot et al. 2018
;
Yang and Hu 2021
)
. Different parametrizations of neural networks lead to different behavior at infinite width
(
Yang and Hu 2021
)
. Here, we will focus on the neural tangent parametrization (NTP), whose infinite-width limit leads to kernel behavior
(
Jacot et al. 2018
)
. While the kernel regime cannot model all aspects of neural network behavior
(
Yehudai and Shamir 2019
;
Ortiz-Jiménez et al. 2021
;
Vyas et al. 2022
;
Wenger et al. 2023
)
, it is one of the models most amenable to theoretical analysis. In particular, at initialization, the function represented by certain infinite-width neural networks is distributed like a Gaussian process with the so-called neural network Gaussian process (NNGP) kernel
(
Neal 1996
;
Daniely et al. 2016
;
Lee et al. 2018
;
Matthews et al. 2018
)
. The training then follows the dynamics of kernel gradient descent with the so-called neural tangent kernel (NTK)
(
Jacot et al. 2018
;
Lee et al. 2019
)
.

To understand the behavior of neural networks in the kernel regime, it is essential to understand the properties of the associated kernels themselves. In particular, the structure of the reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces (RKHSs) corresponding to these kernels is central to further theoretical analysis. Here, we limit ourselves to fully connected neural networks, which are sufficient to study the influence of activation functions, and are still practically relevant by themselves
(
Holzmüller et al. 2024
;
Gorishniy et al. 2025
;
Ye et al. 2024
;
Erickson et al. 2025
)
. While these kernels have been analyzed for (powers of) the ReLU activation and infinitely smooth activation functions
(
Chen and Xu 2021
;
Bietti and Bach 2021
;
Vakili et al. 2023
)
, not much is known about their structure for other activation functions.

Contribution

Our main contribution is
Theorem

9
, illustrated in
10
, where we prove a general result analyzing the structure of the RKHS corresponding to the NTK and NNGP kernels on the sphere
\mathbb{S}^{d}
of fully connected neural networks. Our proof, of which an overview is given in
Appendix

A
, builds upon the analysis of
Bietti and Bach 2021
, but generalizes existing results in multiple ways:

•

We study a significantly larger class of activations. In particular, we provide exact characterizations for typical activation functions that are infinitely smooth everywhere except at zero. We also provide new results for polynomial activation functions, and we more precisely analyze the conditions under which results for infinitely smooth activation functions hold.

•

We study a more general class of fully connected neural networks, covering the cases of normally distributed bias, zero-initialized bias, and no bias. We also study all special cases arising for two-layer networks.

•

We analyze all cases for both NNGP and NTK (except discontinuous activation functions, for which the NTK is not well-defined).

As an additional contribution, in
Theorem

13
, we show how the results on the RKHS translate to the smoothness of functions sampled from the Gaussian process with the NNGP kernel, and hence the smoothness of infinitely wide neural networks at initialization.

After discussing preliminaries (
Section

2
), we introduce our main results in
Section

3
and
Section

4
.
Section

5
discusses some implications of our results on the broader infinite-width theory, such as the (non-)benefit of depth. Related work is discussed in
Section

6
, before
Section

7
concludes with a discussion of possible extensions.

2
Preliminaries

Neural kernels

For
d\geq 1
, we consider fully connected NNs in neural tangent parametrization (NTP) operating on inputs
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{0}},d_{0}\coloneqq d+1
. Typically, neural networks use componentwise-normalized input vectors
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{0}}
such that
\|\boldsymbol{x}\|_{2}\approx\sqrt{d_{0}}
. Following related theoretical literature, we instead consider inputs
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{S}^{d_{0}-1}
and compensate for this by rescaling the first layer by
\sqrt{d_{0}}
, which leads to equivalent learning dynamics with gradient descent.
We consider the following network architecture:

Definition 1
.

Let
L\geq 2
be the number of layers, let
\sigma_{w}>0
, and let
\sigma_{b},\sigma_{i}\geq 0
.
The network is the function
f_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}:\mathbb{R}^{d_{0}}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{L}}
given by
f_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{(0)})\coloneqq\boldsymbol{z}^{(L)}
, where

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}^{(1)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle\sigma_{w}\boldsymbol{W}^{(1)}\boldsymbol{x}^{(0)}+\sigma_{b}\boldsymbol{b}^{(1)}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}^{(l)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma_{w}}{\sqrt{d_{l-1}}}\boldsymbol{W}^{(l)}\boldsymbol{x}^{(l-1)}+\sigma_{b}\boldsymbol{b}^{(l)}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{l}},\qquad\boldsymbol{x}^{(l-1)}\coloneqq\varphi(\boldsymbol{z}^{(l-1)})\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{l}}.\qquad(l\geq 2)

We assume that all parameters are initialized independently as
\boldsymbol{W}^{(l)}_{jk}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
and
b^{(l)}_{j}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{i}^{2})
.
∎

1
subsumes the three cases of

•

bias-free networks by setting
\sigma_{b}=0
,

•

zero-initialized biases by setting
\sigma_{b}\neq 0
,
\sigma_{i}=0
, and

•

randomly initialized biases by setting
\sigma_{b},\sigma_{i}\neq 0
.

For
\sigma_{i}^{2}=1
, the recursive formula for NTK and NNGP kernel are derived in
Lee et al. 2019
.
We derive formulas for the different possible values of
\sigma_{i},\sigma_{b},\sigma_{w}
in
Lemma

D.2
.

To analyze NTK and NNGP kernels, we restrict them to the sphere
\mathbb{S}^{d}
, where they are dot-product kernels due to the rotation-invariance induced by the Gaussian initialization of the weight matrices
\boldsymbol{W}^{(l)}
.

Dot-product kernels on the sphere

Let
\kappa:[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}
such that
k\coloneqq k_{\kappa,d}:\mathbb{S}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d}\to\mathbb{R},(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})\mapsto\kappa(\langle\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\rangle)
is a (positive semidefinite) kernel. Then,
k
is called a
dot-product kernel
. We can leverage the theory of dot-product kernels on spheres to study the RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{k}
associated with
k

(
Bietti and Bach 2021
;
Hubbert et al. 2023
;
Haas et al. 2023
, see e.g.)
: Let
\{Y_{l,1},\ldots,Y_{l,N_{l,d}}\}
be an orthonormal basis of the space of spherical harmonics of degree
l
in
L_{2}(\mathbb{S}^{d})
. The associated integral operator
T_{k}:L_{2}(\mathbb{S}^{d})\to L_{2}(\mathbb{S}^{d})
given by

\displaystyle(T_{k}f)(\boldsymbol{x})=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d}}k(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})f(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}

can be diagonalized as

\displaystyle T_{k}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{l,d}}\mu_{l}Y_{l,i}\langle Y_{l,i},f\rangle_{L_{2}(\mathbb{S}^{d})}\penalty\ .

The RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{k}
associated with
k
can be written as

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{k}=\left\{\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\mu_{l}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{l,d}}a_{l,i}Y_{l,i}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ \sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{l,d}}a_{l,i}^{2}<\infty\right\}

and its inner product as

\displaystyle\left\langle\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\mu_{l}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{l,d}}a_{l,i}Y_{l,i},\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\mu_{l}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{l,d}}b_{l,i}Y_{l,i}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{l,d}}a_{l,i}b_{l,i}\penalty\ .

Note that the eigenfunctions
Y_{l,i}
are the same for all dot-product kernels but the eigenvalues
\mu_{l}=\mu_{l}(k)=\mu_{l}(\kappa,d)
can be different.
Hence, the RKHS is characterized by 1) the asymptotic behavior of the eigenvalues and 2) the set of indices for which the eigenvalues are nonzero.

We introduce some adapted asymptotic notation:

Definition 2

(Asymptotic notation)
.

For functions
\kappa
, we use standard asymptotic notation like
\kappa(t)=O(t^{1/2})
for
t\searrow 0
. For sequences
(a_{n})_{n\in I},(b_{n})_{n\in I}\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
indexed by
I\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we use an index
“
\forall n
”
for asymptotic notation to denote that it should hold for all
n
and not only for almost all
n
, and to indicate the variable
n
that the constant is independent of. Specifically,

\displaystyle a_{n}=O_{\forall n}(b_{n})

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\exists C>0:\forall n\in I:a_{n}\leq Cb_{n}

\displaystyle a_{n}=\Omega_{\forall n}(b_{n})

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle b_{n}=O_{\forall n}(a_{n})

\displaystyle a_{n}=\Theta_{\forall n}(b_{n})

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle a_{n}=O_{\forall n}(b_{n})\text{ and }b_{n}=O_{\forall n}(a_{n})

\displaystyle a_{n}=o_{\forall n}(b_{n})

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle a_{n}=O_{\forall n}(b_{n})\text{ and }\forall C>0:\exists n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}:\forall n\geq n_{0}:a_{n}\leq Cb_{n}

\displaystyle a_{n}=\omega_{\forall n}(b_{n})

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle b_{n}=o_{\forall n}(a_{n})\penalty\ .

Using this notation, when we write
\mu_{l}=\Theta_{\forall l}((l+1)^{-d-2s})
, it implies that all
\mu_{k}
are positive. We write
l+1
and not
l
to make the right-hand side well-defined for all
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. The constant
C
implied in this notation can depend on
d
and
s
.
∎

We call two Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2}

equivalent
if they are equal as sets and their norms are equivalent. Polynomial eigenvalue decays lead to RKHSs equivalent to Sobolev spaces:

Lemma 3

(Sobolev dot-product kernels on the sphere)
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
and
s>d/2
. The RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{k}
corresponding to a kernel
k
on
\mathbb{S}^{d}
is equivalent to the Sobolev space
H^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{d})
if and only if
\mu_{l}=\Theta_{\forall l}((l+1)^{-2s})
.

Proof.

Depending on the definition of
H^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{d})
, this follows either directly or from classical theory, see
Hubbert et al. 2023
and Lemma B.1 in
Haas et al. 2023
.
∎

3
The structure of neural kernels

Before we introduce our main theorem, we need to impose some assumptions on the involved activation functions.

Definition 4

(Functions with polynomially bounded derivatives)
.

Let
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
be an interval. Then, we define the set
\mathcal{S}^{(\infty)}(I)
to contain all functions
\varphi_{I}\in C^{\infty}(I)
with polynomially bounded derivatives. In other words: For all
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, there exist
a_{m},b_{m},q_{m}>0
such that for all
x\in I
,
|\varphi_{I}^{(m)}(x)|\leq a_{m}|x|^{q_{m}}+b_{m}
.
∎

Polynomial boundedness for
m\in\{0,1\}
is required to get the NTK formulas from
(
Yang 2020
, Theorem 7.2, Box 1)
; for higher derivatives we only need
\varphi_{I}^{(m)}\in L_{2}(\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}))
for all
\sigma^{2}>0
.

Assumption 5

(Activation function)
.

We assume that
\varphi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is of the form
1
1

1

The definition at zero ensures that
\varphi
is continuous when
\varphi_{+}
and
\varphi_{-}
allow, and that its even/odd parts are zero everywhere whenever they are zero almost everywhere.

\displaystyle\varphi(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\varphi_{+}(x)&,x>0\\
\varphi_{-}(x)&,x<0\\
\frac{1}{2}(\lim_{x\searrow 0}\varphi_{+}(x)+\lim_{x\nearrow 0}\varphi_{-}(x))&,x=0\end{cases}

with
\varphi_{-}\in\mathcal{S}^{(\infty)}((-\infty,0))
and
\varphi_{+}\in\mathcal{S}^{(\infty)}((0,\infty))
and that
\varphi
is not the zero function.
∎

Proposition 6

(All common activation function satisfy
5
)
.

1.

If
f
is constructed by addition, multiplication, and composition of polynomials,
\sigmoid,\tanh,\softplus,\sin,\cos,\RBF
and
\Phi
, then
f\in\mathcal{S}^{(\infty)}(\mathbb{R})
. Moreover, the functions
g(x)=\exp(ax)
are in
\mathcal{S}^{(\infty)}((-\infty,0])
for every
a\geq 0
.

2.

5
is satisfied for all activation functions from
Table

1
. It is also satisfied for all
\phi\in\mathcal{S}^{(\infty)}
.

We prove
Proposition

6
in
Appendix

E
.

Definition 7

(Smoothness of an activation function)
.

For an activation function
\varphi
as in
5
, we define its smoothness as

\displaystyle\operatorname{smoothness}(\varphi)

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle\inf\{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\mid\lim_{t\searrow 0}\varphi^{(m)}(t)\neq\lim_{t\nearrow 0}\varphi^{(m)}(t)\}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\cup\{\infty\}.

∎

Activation functions and their smoothness (
7
).
Here,
\mathcal{P}_{m}
means that the function is a polynomial of degree
m
.
\mathcal{P}_{-\infty}
means that the function is zero. For a more extensive overview, we refer to
Dubey et al. 2022
.

Example 8

(Smoothness of the ReLU activation)
.

The ReLU activation function
\varphi(x)=\max\{0,x\}
fulfills
5
and decomposes as
\varphi_{+}(x)=x\ ,x>0
,
\varphi_{-}(x)=0\ ,x<0
. Hence we have

\displaystyle\lim_{t\searrow 0}\varphi(t)=0=\lim_{t\nearrow 0}\varphi(t)\qquad\text{but}\qquad\lim_{t\searrow 0}\varphi^{\prime}(t)=1\neq 0=\lim_{t\nearrow 0}\varphi^{\prime}(t)\penalty\ ,

implying
\operatorname{smoothness}(\mathrm{ReLU})=1
.
∎

We now introduce our main result, which contains quite some special cases. The
“regular”
cases are discussed afterward in
10
. These special cases in the bias-free case involve the common even/odd decomposition

\displaystyle\varphi_{\mathrm{even}}(x)\coloneqq\frac{\varphi(x)+\varphi(-x)}{2}\penalty\ ,\quad\varphi_{\mathrm{odd}}(x)\coloneqq\frac{\varphi(x)-\varphi(-x)}{2}\penalty\ ,

fulfilling
\varphi=\varphi_{\mathrm{even}}+\varphi_{\mathrm{odd}}
. We additionally define

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\text{even/odd}}\coloneqq\{f:\mathbb{S}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}\mid f\text{ is even/odd}\}\penalty\ ,

where the context determines the parity even/odd. For example,
\mathrm{ReLU}_{\mathrm{even}}(x)=\frac{1}{2}|x|
and
\mathrm{ReLU}_{\mathrm{even}}(x)=\frac{1}{2}x
. The function
f_{\varphi}
of a two-layer network with activation
\varphi
can be written as
f_{\varphi}=f_{\varphi_{\mathrm{even}}}+f_{\varphi_{\mathrm{odd}}}
, and in the bias-free case (
\sigma_{b}^{2}=0
),
f_{\varphi_{\mathrm{even}}}
is even and
f_{\varphi_{\mathrm{odd}}}
is odd. Hence,
(f_{\mathrm{ReLU}})_{\mathrm{odd}}=f_{\mathrm{ReLU}_{\mathrm{odd}}}
is a linear function, which explains the results of
Bietti and Mairal 2019
where the odd eigenvalues satisfy
\mu_{1}>0
,
\mu_{3}=\mu_{5}=\ldots=0
. A much more general version of this calculation is performed in
Proposition

D.4
.

By
\mathcal{P}_{m}
we denote the polynomials of degree at most
m
. We say that two Hilbert spaces
H_{1},H_{2}
are equivalent, or
H_{1}\cong H_{2}
, if they are equal as sets and their norms are equivalent.

Theorem 9

(Main result, summary below)
.

Let the activation
\varphi
fulfill
5
.

(NNGP)

(a)

If
\sigma_{b}^{2}\sigma_{i}^{2}>0
or (
L\geq 3
and
\varphi
is neither even nor odd), let
s\coloneqq\operatorname{smoothness}(\varphi)
.

i.

If
s=0
, then
\mathcal{H}_{k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L}}\cong H^{d/2+2^{1-L}}(\mathbb{S}^{d})
.

ii.

If
1\leq s<\infty
, then
\mathcal{H}_{k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L}}\cong H^{d/2+s+1/2}(\mathbb{S}^{d})
.

iii.

If
s=\infty
and
\varphi
is not a polynomial, then
\mathcal{H}_{k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L}}\subset H^{t}(\mathbb{S}^{d})
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
H_{k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L}}
contains all polynomials.

iv.

If
s=\infty
and
\varphi
is a polynomial of degree
m
, then
\mathcal{H}_{k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L}}\cong\mathcal{P}_{m^{L-1}}
.

(b)

If
\sigma_{b}^{2}\sigma_{i}^{2}=0
and
\varphi
is even/odd, let
s\coloneqq\operatorname{smoothness}(\varphi)
.

i.

If
s=0
, then
\mathcal{H}_{k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L}}\cong H^{d/2+2^{1-L}}(\mathbb{S}^{d})\cap\mathcal{F}_{\text{even/odd}}
.

ii.

If
1\leq s<\infty
, then
\mathcal{H}_{k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L}}\cong H^{d/2+s+1/2}(\mathbb{S}^{d})\cap\mathcal{F}_{\text{even/odd}}
.

iii.

If
s=\infty
and
\varphi
is not a polynomial, then
\mathcal{H}_{k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L}}\subset H^{t}(\mathbb{S}^{d})\cap\mathcal{F}_{\text{even/odd}}
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
H_{k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L}}
contains all even/odd polynomials.

iv.

If
s=\infty
and
\varphi
is a polynomial of degree
m
, then

\mathcal{H}_{k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L}}\cong\mathcal{P}_{m^{L-1}}\cap\mathcal{F}_{\text{even/odd}}
.

(c)

If
\sigma_{b}^{2}\sigma_{i}^{2}=0
,
L=2
, and
\varphi
is not even or odd, then

\mathcal{H}_{k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L}}\cong H_{k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{\varphi_{\mathrm{even}},L}}\oplus H_{k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{\varphi_{\mathrm{odd}},L}}
where the RKHSs
\mathcal{H}_{k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{\varphi_{\mathrm{even}},L}},\mathcal{H}_{k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{\varphi_{\mathrm{odd}},L}}
are corresponding to the even/odd activation functions
\varphi_{\mathrm{even}}
or respectively
\varphi_{\mathrm{odd}}
and are given in case ii).

(NTK)

(a)

If
\sigma_{b}^{2}>0
or (
L\geq 3
and
\varphi
is neither even nor odd), let
s\coloneqq\operatorname{smoothness}(\varphi)
.

i.

If
1\leq s<\infty
, then
\mathcal{H}_{k^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{L}}\cong H^{d/2+s-1/2}(\mathbb{S}^{d})
.

ii.

If
s=\infty
and
\varphi
is not a polynomial, then
\mathcal{H}_{k^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{L}}\subset H^{t}(\mathbb{S}^{d})
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
H_{k^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{L}}
contains all polynomials.

iii.

If
s=\infty
and
\varphi
is a polynomial of degree
m
, then

\mathcal{H}_{k^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{L}}\cong\mathcal{P}_{m^{L-1}}
.

(b)

If
\sigma_{b}^{2}=0
and
\varphi
is even/odd, let
s\coloneqq\operatorname{smoothness}(\varphi)
.

i.

If
1\leq s<\infty
, then
\mathcal{H}_{k^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{L}}\cong H^{d/2+s-1/2}(\mathbb{S}^{d})\cap\mathcal{F}_{\text{even/odd}}
.

ii.

If
s=\infty
and
\varphi
is not a polynomial, then
\mathcal{H}_{k^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{L}}\subset H^{t}(\mathbb{S}^{d})\cap\mathcal{F}_{\text{even/odd}}
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
H_{k^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{L}}
contains all even/odd polynomials.

iii.

If
s=\infty
and
\varphi
is a polynomial of degree
m
, then
\mathcal{H}_{k^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{L}}=\mathcal{P}_{m^{L-1}}\cap\mathcal{F}_{\text{even/odd}}
.

(c)

If
\sigma_{b}^{2}=0
,
L=2
, and
\varphi
is not even or odd, then

\mathcal{H}_{k^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{L}}\cong H_{k^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{\varphi_{\mathrm{even}},L}}\oplus H_{k^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{\varphi_{\mathrm{odd}},L}}
where the RKHSs
\mathcal{H}_{k^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{\varphi_{\mathrm{even}},L}},\mathcal{H}_{k^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{\varphi_{\mathrm{odd}},L}}
are corresponding to the even/odd activation functions
\varphi_{\mathrm{even}}
or respectively
\varphi_{\mathrm{odd}}
and are given in case ii).

Note that
H^{t}(\mathbb{S}^{d})\cap\mathcal{F}_{\text{even/odd}}
is a closed subspace of
H^{t}(\mathbb{S}^{d})
for any
t
. We equip it with the restricted norm.

Proof.

Lemma

D.2
yields the convergence of the NNGP- and NTK-kernels.

Theorem

D.10
combined with
Lemma

3
and a careful case distinction then yields the claim.
∎

Note that the results of
Theorem

9
can be expressed in terms of the eigenvalues
\mu_{l}
of the integral operator instead of the RKHSs as discussed in
Section

2
; the eigenvalue-based formulation can be found in
Theorem

D.10
.

Remark 10

(General takeaway)
.

Theorem

9
is most easy to understand for the case where

•

the NN has
L\geq 2
layers, contains biases (
\sigma_{b}^{2}>0
), and in the NNGP case the biases are initialized with nonzero variance (
\sigma_{i}^{2}>0
), or

•

the NN has
L\geq 3
layers and the activation function
\varphi
is neither even nor odd.

In this case, the decay of the eigenvalues
\mu_{l}
and the corresponding RKHS depend on the smoothness
s
of the activation function
\varphi
as follows:

•

If the activation function is a polynomial, for NTK and NNGP only finitely many eigenvalues
\mu_{l}
are nonzero and the RKHSs only contain polynomials, whose maximum degree grows exponentially with the depth of the network.

•

If the activation function is infinitely smooth (
s=\infty
) but not a polynomial, all
\mu_{l}
are nonzero but decay faster than any inverse polynomial. Hence, the RKHSs are contained in all Sobolev spaces, but the kernels are universal. This applies for example to the GELU, SiLU/Swish, Mish, softplus, sigmoid, and tanh activation functions (though tanh is an odd function, so the conclusion only holds with biases).

•

If the activation function has finite smoothness
1\leq s<\infty
, the RKHSs are equivalent to Sobolev spaces of order
d/2+s-1/2
for the NTK and
d/2+s+1/2
for the NNGP. This applies to ReLU, LeakyReLU, ELU (
\alpha\neq 1
) and SELU with
s=1
and to CELU with
s=2
.
Rectified power unit activations
\max\{0,x\}^{s}
have smoothness
s\in\mathbb{N}
, yielding a family of Sobolev spaces. Our results show that this is due to the behavior at zero and that the superlinear growth of these functions for
s\geq 2
is not necessary.

•

If the activation function is discontinuous (
s=0
), the RKHS of the NNGP kernel is equivalent to a Sobolev space of smoothness
d/2+2^{1-L}
, while the NTK is not defined. ∎

Example 11

(Special cases)
.

As mentioned above, two-layer networks without bias (
\sigma_{b}^{2}=0
for NTK or
\sigma_{b}^{2}\sigma_{i}^{2}=0
for NNGP) can have special behavior. Following
Table

1
, we obtain for CELU the NTK RKHS
(H^{d/2+5/2}\cap\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{even}})\oplus(H^{d/2+3/2}\cap\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{odd}})
, so the even parts of functions are smoother than the odd parts of functions. For ELU (
\alpha\neq 1
) and SELU, we obtain
(H^{d/2+1/2}\cap\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{even}})\oplus(H^{d/2+3/2}\cap\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{odd}})
. For ReLU and LeakyReLU, the odd parts are just linear functions. For RePU, one of the two parts are polynomials up to a certain degree.

The other special case is for even or odd activations and arbitrarily deep networks without bias. For example,
\tanh
is an odd activation, hence the corresponding NNGP and NTK RKHSs will only contain odd functions.
∎

Remark 12

(NTKs and NNGPs of non-smooth activations are equivalent to Matérn kernels)
.

Chen and Xu 2021
and
Bietti and Bach 2021
showed that on
\mathbb{S}^{d}
, the RKHS of the deep ReLU NTK is equivalent to that of the Laplace kernel. Theorem 1 in
Vakili et al. 2021
further shows that the RKHSs of deep RePU NTKs and NNGPs are equivalent to the ones of Matérn kernels, which are equivalent to Sobolev spaces. Specifically, for the Matérn kernel
k_{\nu}
of order
\nu>0
on the sphere
\mathbb{S}^{d}
, it holds that
\mathcal{H}_{k_{\nu}}\cong H^{d/2+\nu}(\mathbb{S}^{d})
(see also Proposition 5.2 (c) in
Hubbert et al. 2023
). For other not-infinitely-smooth activations, our
Theorem

9
shows that their RKHSs are generally also equivalent to Sobolev spaces, and hence the corresponding kernels are equivalent to Matérn kernels.
∎

4
Smoothness of NNGP sample paths

Theorem 13
.

Let
k
be a dot-product kernel on
\mathbb{S}^{d}
whose RKHS is equivalent to a Sobolev space
H^{d+\alpha}(\mathbb{S}^{d})
,
\alpha>0
. Let
X
be a Gaussian process on
\mathbb{S}^{d}
with zero mean and covariance kernel
k
.

1.

For any
\varepsilon>0
we have
P(Y\in H^{\alpha+d/2+\varepsilon}(\mathbb{S}^{d}))=0
for any version
Y
of
X
.

2.

For any
0<\varepsilon<\alpha
, there exists a version
Y
of
X
such that
\mathbb{P}\left(Y\in H^{d/2+\alpha-\varepsilon}\right)=1
holds.

Theorem

13
is proven in
Appendix

F
. Note that
H^{d/2+\alpha-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{S}^{d})\hookrightarrow C(\mathbb{S}^{d})
holds for
0<\varepsilon<\alpha
, i.e., the embedding
\id:H^{d/2+\alpha-\varepsilon}\to C(\mathbb{S}^{d}),f\mapsto f
is continuous.

Example 14

(Application to infinite-width networks)
.

Consider for simplicity the case with biases (
\sigma_{b}^{2}\sigma_{i}^{2}>0
) and an activation of smoothness
1\leq s<\infty
. From
Theorem

9
we know
\mathcal{H}_{\kappa^{\mathrm{NTK}}}\cong H^{d/2+s-1/2}(\mathbb{S}^{d})
and
\mathcal{H}_{\kappa^{\mathrm{NNGP}}}\cong H^{d/2+s+1/2}(\mathbb{S}^{d})
. If
s+1/2>d/2
, by
Theorem

13
, the NNGP sample paths are in
H^{s+1/2-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{S}^{d})
but not
H^{s+1/2+\varepsilon}(\mathbb{S}^{d})
for any
\varepsilon>0
. We conjecture that randomly initialized finite-width networks are only in
H^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{d})
but not
H^{s+\varepsilon}(\mathbb{S}^{d})
, so the infinite-width limit would gain an extra half-order of smoothness.
∎

5
Implications

Our results can be used to obtain a more complete understanding of neural networks in the kernel regime. Below, we list some non-exhaustive ways in which our results could complement existing theory.

By showing that the RKHSs of deep and shallow ReLU NTKs are equivalent,
Bietti and Bach 2021
concluded that the NTK regime is not sufficient to model the benefits of depth for practical neural networks. Our results show that this holds for a much larger class of not infinitely smooth activation functions. On the other hand, our results show that the RKHSs are not equivalent for polynomial activations, while the situation for other infinitely smooth activations remains unclear, as more precise results are only known in the two-layer case
(
Murray et al. 2023
)
.

Regarding theoretical modeling, the dynamics of gradient flow are difficult to study for ReLU activations since their derivative is discontinuous at zero. The use of smoother but not infinitely smooth activations such as CELU can enable theoretical studies of gradient flow in a setting where the RKHS is well-known, without limiting results to powers of ReLU as in previous work
(
Vakili et al. 2023
)
. For example, the gradient flow analysis of
Bowman and Montufar 2022
yields finite-time bounds for the deviation from the infinite-width training trajectory but requires activation functions smoother than ReLU. The use of semi-smooth activation functions might also be interesting to the NTK analysis of physics-informed neural networks
(
Wang et al. 2022
)
, since their training involves taking higher-order derivatives of neural networks.

Our main result allows the application of generalization results to more activation functions. For example, the inconsistency results of
Haas et al. 2023
for overfitting with NTKs and NNGPs apply whenever the RKHS is a Sobolev space, and hence by our results, they apply to a wide range of finitely smooth activation functions.
Our results also suggest that the spectral bias of neural networks
(
Rahaman et al. 2019
)
can be influenced by changing the smoothness of the activation function. Finally, while
Simon et al. 2022
show that a large class of kernels can be represented as NTKs and NNGPs, our results help to elucidate the properties of activation functions that realize these kernels.

Compared to the pure kernel case, gradient flow on infinite-width NNs in the kernel regime also has to learn to remove the random initial function
(
Lee et al. 2019
)
. Thanks to
Theorem

13
, we now know the smoothness of this function, which makes it possible to derive convergence rates for the regression of this function with the NTK. To this end, we refer to the proof of Theorem G.5 in
Haas et al. 2023
for convergence rates with regularization in the noisy case, and to Theorem 3.3 in
Le Gia et al. 2006
for an approach towards convergence rates of interpolation.

6
Related work

Neural kernels

Multiple results have shown that in typical settings, infinite-width neural networks behave like Gaussian processes at initialization
(
Neal 1996
;
Daniely et al. 2016
;
Lee et al. 2018
;
Matthews et al. 2018
)
. The associated covariance function is known as the neural network Gaussian process (NNGP) kernel or random features kernel.
Jacot et al. 2018
discovered that the training dynamics of such infinite-width neural networks can be described by a different kernel, the so-called neural tangent kernel (NTK). The same observation has been made by
Lee et al. 2019
. Follow-up work has generalized the scope of these results
(
Arora et al. 2019
;
Yang 2019b
;
Yang 2020
, e.g.,)
. These works do not yet give deep insights into the nature of the NTK and NNGP kernels.

Smoothness of neural kernels

Belkin et al. 2018
noted similarities in generalization behavior between the Laplace kernel and neural networks. A follow-up line of work managed to characterize the RKHS of different NTKs on
\mathbb{S}^{d}
.
Bietti and Mairal 2019
derived the structure of the RKHS of the two-layer ReLU NTK without bias (
\sigma_{b}^{2}=0
).
Basri et al. 2019
analyzed two-layer ReLU NTKs with and without bias.
Geifman et al. 2020
showed that the two-layer ReLU NTK with bias is equivalent to the Laplace kernel, and showed that the RKHS of the deep ReLU NTK is at least as large as the RKHS of the shallow one. The equivalence of these RKHSs was then shown by
Chen and Xu 2021
for
\sigma_{b}^{2}>0,\sigma_{i}=0,L\geq 2
. Simultaneously,
Bietti and Bach 2021
characterized the structure of the deep ReLU NTK and NNGP without bias (
\sigma_{b}^{2}=0,L\geq 3
) and also showed some results for step activations as well as infinitely differentiable activations.
Vakili et al. 2023
extended this analysis to RePU (integer powers of the ReLU activation). Finally,
Haas et al. 2023
formally established the connection of these RKHSs to Sobolev spaces on the sphere.
Murray et al. 2023
prove further spectral properties of the NTK, including a characterization of the RKHS of two-layer NNs for tanh and RBF activation functions that is more precise than our result for these activations.
Scetbon and Harchaoui 2021
prove some results about spectral properties of dot-product kernels, with weak results concerning the NTK. However, to the best of our knowledge, we are the first to provide a characterization of the RKHS for many non-smooth activation functions like LeakyReLU, SELU, or ELU.

The NTK has also been analyzed for other architectures such as residual networks
(
Belfer et al. 2024
)
and convolutional residual networks
(
Barzilai et al. 2023
)
.
Dandi and Jacot 2021
investigate the contributions of individual layers towards the properties of the NTK.

Simon et al. 2022
show that a large class of dot-product kernels on the sphere can be realized as NTK or NNGP kernels through suitable activation functions.
While most results analyze the NTK on
\mathbb{S}^{d}
,
Lai et al. 2023
analyze the eigenvalue decay on
\mathbb{R}
and
Li et al. 2024
leverage known results on
\mathbb{S}^{d}
to obtain decay rates for
\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
. In terms of activation functions, these results do not go beyond the known results for
\mathbb{S}^{d}
.

Spectral properties

Panigrahi et al. 2019
investigate minimum eigenvalues of NTK matrices and find a dependence on the smoothness of the activation function.
Nguyen et al. 2021
and
Karhadkar et al. 2024
provide more bounds for minimum eigenvalues. These results are related to the structure of the RKHS through kernel matrix concentration inequalities but do not reveal the full structure of the RKHS.

7
Conclusion

We have shown general results for the structure of the RKHS for fully connected neural networks with different activation functions, as well as results for the sample path smoothness of randomly initialized wide neural networks. We refer the reader to
Appendix

A
for a short overview of the central techniques and objects of our main proof.

Possible extensions

Our work offers several possibilities for extensions. First, following
Li et al. 2024
, an extension of our results from
x\in\mathbb{S}^{d}
to
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
could be possible. Second, one could study different activation functions in different layers; we strongly conjecture that the behavior of the NTK and NNGP will be dominated by the least smooth activation function. Third, while we study non-smoothness in zero, it might be possible to apply our proof technique to functions that are non-smooth in other points—the main obstacle is to find reference functions
s_{k}
with the given non-smoothness that are similarly amenable to analysis as the functions
s_{k}
we use in the appendix. Fourth, a more precise characterization of the RKHS for infinitely smooth non-polynomial activations is still open but might require stronger tools
(
Minh et al. 2006
;
Azevedo and Menegatto 2014
;
Murray et al. 2023
, e.g.,)
. Finally, our results might be extensible to analyses for residual networks
(
Belfer et al. 2024
;
Tirer et al. 2022
, e.g.,)
, convolutional networks
(
Geifman et al. 2022
;
Barzilai et al. 2023
, e.g.,)
, or more general architectures including transformers
(
Yang 2020
)
.

Contribution statement

D.H. conceived the project and the high-level proof strategy. D.H. and M.S. contributed to proof writing and paper writing.

Appendix A
Overview and notation

Appendix

A
–
D
are dedicated to the proof of the main theorem,
Theorem

9
. They build on each other and should be read in order.
Appendix

E
and
Appendix

F
contain smaller proofs that are independent of the other appendices.

Analysis through boundary behavior.

As mentioned before, the NTK and NNGP kernels on the sphere are dot-product kernels, i.e., they are of the form
k(x,x^{\prime})=\kappa(\langle x,x^{\prime}\rangle)
. To obtain eigenvalue decays for
k
, we build on the results of
Bietti and Bach 2021
, which requires to study the behavior of
\kappa(t)
for
t\to 1
and
t\to-1
. To unify these analyses, we let
\tau\in\{-1,1\}
and study the behavior of
\kappa_{\tau}:(0,2)\to\mathbb{R},t\mapsto\kappa(\tau(1-t))
for
t\searrow 0
. We first introduce function classes with certain boundary behavior that will be central in simplifying computations later on:

Definition A.1

(Function classes with controlled boundary behavior)
.

Let
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
. We define sets of functions
\mathcal{P}_{\alpha,\beta}
,
\mathcal{R}_{\gamma}
, and
\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\beta}
as follows:

1.

Sums of high-enough powers:
We define
\mathcal{P}_{\alpha,\beta}
as the set of functions
f:(0,2)\to\mathbb{R}
of the form

\displaystyle f(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}t^{\alpha_{i}}\quad\text{with}\quad n\in\mathbb{N}_{0},a_{i},\alpha_{i}\in\mathbb{R},\begin{cases}\alpha_{i}>\alpha&\text{for all $i$ with }\alpha_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}\\
\alpha_{i}>\beta&\text{for all $i$ with }\alpha_{i}\not\in\mathbb{Z}\end{cases}

Note the use of
>
, which will be convenient later.

2.

Negligible remainder functions:
We define
\mathcal{R}_{\gamma}
as the set of
C^{\infty}
-functions
f:(0,2)\to\mathbb{R}
such that for all
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, the
m
-th derivative
f^{(m)}
satisfies

\displaystyle|f^{(m)}(t)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle O(t^{\gamma-m})\quad\text{ for }t\searrow 0\penalty\ .

Intuitively,
\mathcal{R}_{\gamma}
contains functions that are at least as benign as
t\mapsto t^{\gamma}
, in terms of the behavior of all derivatives for
t\to 0
.

3.

Sums of powers plus negligible remainder:
We define
\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\beta}\coloneqq\bigcap_{\gamma\in\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{P}_{\alpha,\beta}+\mathcal{R}_{\gamma})
. In other words,
\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\beta}
contains those functions that are sums of powers plus an arbitrarily
“high-order”
remainder. Note for
f\in\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\beta}
that in general, the number
n
of terms in the sum of powers when writing
f
as
f\in\mathcal{P}_{\alpha,\beta}+\mathcal{R}_{\gamma}
will depend on the imposed order
\gamma
of the remainder.

We will mostly use
\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\beta}
in the paper. Instead of
f\in\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\beta}
, we also write
f(t)=\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\beta}(t)
analogous to common
O
-notation. We often track coefficients of leading terms separately, e.g., writing
g(t)=at^{\alpha}+bt^{\beta}+\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\beta}(t)
.
∎

We build on the following result, formulated using the previously defined boundary function classes:

Theorem A.2

(Adaptation of Theorem 7 in arXiv v4 of
Bietti and Bach 2021
)
.

Let
\kappa:[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}
be a function that is smooth on
(-1,1)
such that
k_{\kappa,d}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})=\kappa(\langle\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\rangle)
is a positive semi-definite kernel on all spheres
\mathbb{S}^{d},d\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
. Suppose that there exists
0<\beta\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Z}
and
b_{-1},b_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
such that for
\tau\in\{-1,1\}
,

\displaystyle\kappa(\tau(1-t))=b_{\tau}t^{\beta}+\mathcal{Q}_{-1,\beta}(t)\penalty\ .

Then, for a given dimension
d\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
, the eigenvalues
\mu_{l}=\mu_{l}(\kappa,d)
as defined in
Section

2
satisfy:

1.

For
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
even, if
b_{-1}\neq-b_{1}
, then
\mu_{l}=\Theta_{\forall l}((l+1)^{-d-2\beta})
.

2.

For
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
even, if
b_{-1}=-b_{1}
, then
\mu_{l}=o_{\forall l}((l+1)^{-d-2\beta})
.

3.

For
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
odd, if
b_{-1}\neq b_{1}
, then
\mu_{l}=\Theta_{\forall l}((l+1)^{-d-2\beta})
.

4.

For
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
odd, if
b_{-1}=b_{1}
, then
\mu_{l}=o_{\forall l}((l+1)^{-d-2\beta})
.

This leads to the following strategy, detailed below, for analyzing the eigenvalues of neural kernels on the sphere:

1.

Write neural kernels on the sphere as a composition of dual activation functions.

2.

Analyze the boundary behavior of dual activation functions.

3.

Analyze the boundary behavior for sums, products, and compositions of functions, which will be done in
Proposition

B.2
.

4.

Analyze the behavior of even and odd functions to obtain stronger results for the special cases (b) and (d) in
Theorem

A.2
.

5.

Assemble everything.

Dual activations and neural kernels.

To apply the theorem above, similar to
Bietti and Bach 2021
, we derive a convenient expression for
\kappa
using
dual activation functions
introduced by
Daniely et al. 2016
.

Definition A.3

(Dual and rescaled activation functions)
.

For a function
\varphi\in L_{2}(\mathcal{N}(0,1))
, we follow
Daniely et al. 2016
and define the dual activation

\displaystyle\hbox{\hskip 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 411.22394pt}:[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R},t\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{(u,v)\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{t})}[\varphi(u)\varphi(v)],\qquad\Sigma_{t}\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}1&t\\
t&1\end{pmatrix}\penalty\ .

Moreover, for a function
\varphi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
and
a\in\mathbb{R}
, we define the rescaled function

\displaystyle\varphi_{\cdot a}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R},\varphi_{\cdot a}(x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle\varphi(ax)\penalty\ .

∎

Our following result, derived in
Section

D.1
, shows that the NTK and NNGP kernels restricted to the sphere can be expressed using dual rescaled activations:

Lemma A.4

(Neural kernels on the unit sphere)
.

Let the activation function
\varphi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
fulfill
5
.
Consider a neural network
f_{\theta}:\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\to\mathbb{R}
initialized as in
1
.
For
l\geq 2
and
t\in[-1,1]
we recursively define

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle\sigma_{b}^{2}\sigma_{i}^{2}+\sigma_{w}^{2}

\displaystyle\alpha_{l}

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle\sigma_{b}^{2}\sigma_{i}^{2}+\sigma_{w}^{2}\hbox{\hskip 401.59273pt\hskip-11.64052pt\hbox{$\varphi_{\cdot\sqrt{\alpha_{l-1}}}$}\hskip-11.64052pt\hskip-401.59273pt\raisebox{-0.65001pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.11778pt}{\resizebox{47060480}{677201}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern \alpha_{l}>0
and

\displaystyle k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{l}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\kappa^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{l}(\langle\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\rangle)

\displaystyle k^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{l}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\kappa^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{l}(\langle\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\rangle)\penalty\ .

Boundary behavior of dual activations.

To study the behavior of dual activations at
\pm 1
, we first need a definition:

Definition A.5

(Reference activations)
.

For
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we define the activation functions
s_{k}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle s_{k}(x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2k!}\sgn(x)x^{k}\penalty\ ,\qquad\sgn(x)\coloneqq\begin{cases}-1&,x<0\\
0&,x=0\\
1&,x>0\penalty\ .\end{cases}

Let
\varphi
be an activation function as in
5
. For
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we define the coefficients

\displaystyle\Delta_{k}(\varphi)\coloneqq\varphi^{(k)}(0+)-\varphi^{(k)}(0-)\penalty\ ,

where
\varphi^{(k)}(0+)
is the right-sided limit of the
k
-th derivative of
\varphi
at zero, and
\varphi^{(k)}(0-)
is the left-sided limit.
∎

The motivation behind
A.5
is given in
Lemma

C.15
, which says that we can decompose

\displaystyle\varphi=\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\Delta_{k}(\varphi)s_{k}+\varphi_{m}\penalty\ ,

(A.1)

where
\varphi_{m}
is
m
times pseudo-differentiable (see
C.1
). This decomposition is central to our proof.

As in
Daniely et al. 2016
, we use
h_{n}
to denote the
n
-th normalized probabilist’s Hermite polynomial, which means that
h_{n}
is a polynomial of degree
n
, is even for even
n
and odd for odd
n
, and
(h_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
forms an orthonormal basis of the Hilbert space
L_{2}(\mathcal{N}(0,1))
. For a function
f\in L_{2}(\mathcal{N}(0,1))
and
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we define its
n
-th Hermite coefficient

\displaystyle a_{n}(f)\coloneqq\langle f,h_{n}\rangle_{L_{2}(\mathcal{N}(0,1))}\penalty\ ,

such that we have the following Hermite expansion in
L_{2}(\mathcal{N}(0,1))
:

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}(f)h_{n}\penalty\ .

Daniely et al. 2016
show that the Hermite series of
\varphi
is related to the Maclaurin series of

\varphi

\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt

via

\displaystyle\hbox{\hskip 411.22394pt\hskip-3.27083pt\hbox{$\varphi$}\hskip-3.27083pt\hskip-411.22394pt\raisebox{-0.65001pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.11778pt}{\resizebox{48365776}{677201}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94691pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-411.22394pt\hskip 411.22394pt}(t)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}(\varphi)^{2}t^{n}\penalty\ .

(A.2)

This also demonstrates that the
“dualization”
of activation functions should be thought of as a (function-valued) quadratic form and not dualization. Using our decomposition in Eq. (
A.1
), we therefore obtain

\displaystyle\hbox{\hskip 411.22394pt\hskip-3.27083pt\hbox{$\varphi$}\hskip-3.27083pt\hskip-411.22394pt\raisebox{-0.65001pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.11778pt}{\resizebox{48365776}{677201}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern \displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{m}\sum_{j=0}^{m}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\Delta_{i}(\varphi)\Delta_{j}(\varphi)a_{n}(s_{i})a_{n}(s_{j})t^{n}

(A.3)

\displaystyle\penalty\ +\penalty\ \sum_{i=0}^{m}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}2\Delta_{i}(\varphi)a_{n}(s_{i})a_{n}(\varphi_{m})t^{n}+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}(\varphi_{m})^{2}t^{n}\penalty\ .

We will treat the first term involving
a_{n}(s_{i})a_{n}(s_{j})
analytically, while using the smoothness of
\varphi_{m}
to obtain fast decay rates for
a_{n}(\varphi_{m})
, which will imply that the behavior at the boundary is dominated by the first term. In total, we obtain the following result:

Theorem A.6

(Boundary behavior of dual activations)
.

Let
\varphi
be an activation function as in
5
and let
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that
\Delta_{k}(\varphi)=0
for all
k<m
(see
A.5
). Then, there exists
b_{m}>0
such that for
\tau\in\{\pm 1\}
,

\displaystyle\hbox{\hskip 401.59273pt\hskip-3.27083pt\hbox{$\varphi$}\hskip-3.27083pt\hskip-401.59273pt\raisebox{-0.65001pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.11778pt}{\resizebox{47060480}{677201}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern \displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta_{m}(\varphi)^{2}(-\tau)^{m+1}b_{m}t^{m+1/2}+\mathcal{Q}_{-1,m+1/2}(t)

holds.

To deal with the derivative of the activation in the NTK, we can use the convenient formula
\hbox{\hskip 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-411.22394pt\hskip 411.22394pt}=\hbox{\hskip 411.22394pt\hskip-3.27083pt\hbox{$\varphi$}\hskip-3.27083pt\hskip-411.22394pt\raisebox{-0.65001pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.11778pt}{\resizebox{48365776}{677201}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94691pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94725pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.97221pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-411.22394pt\hskip 411.22394pt}^{\prime}
from
Daniely et al. 2016
, of which a generalized formulation (for pseudo-derivatives, see
C.1
) is proved in
Lemma

C.2
.

Even and odd functions.

Let
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
be symmetric around zero and let
f:I\to\mathbb{R}
be a function. We say that
f
is even if
f(x)=f(-x)
for all
x\in I
, and that
f
is odd if
f(x)=-f(-x)
for all
x\in I
. Every function
f:I\to\mathbb{R}
can be decomposed in its even and odd part

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{even}}(x)=\frac{f(x)+f(-x)}{2},\qquad f_{\mathrm{odd}}(x)=\frac{f(x)-f(-x)}{2}\penalty\ .

We then have
f=f_{\mathrm{even}}+f_{\mathrm{odd}}
,
f_{\mathrm{even}}
is even, and
f_{\mathrm{odd}}
is odd.

For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we define

\displaystyle\even(n)\coloneqq\begin{cases}1&,n\text{ is even}\\
0&,n\text{ is odd,}\end{cases}\qquad\odd(n)\coloneqq\begin{cases}0&,n\text{ is even}\\
1&,n\text{ is odd,}\end{cases}

The Hermite polynomials
h_{n}
are even for even
n
and odd for odd
n
.

We show in
Proposition

D.4
that two-layer bias-free neural kernels satisfy an even-odd decomposition, and deeper bias-free neural kernels can be even/odd whenever the activation function is even/odd.

Appendix B
Boundary behavior

In the following, we will prove some rules for algebraic manipulations with the function classes
\mathcal{P}_{\alpha,\beta},\mathcal{R}_{\gamma},\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\beta}
from
A.1
. While we mostly care about
\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\beta}
later, we will first prove rules for
\mathcal{P}_{\alpha,\beta}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\gamma}
as a simpler intermediate step, before providing rules for
\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\beta}
in
Proposition

B.2
, which are summarized in
Table

B.1
:

Lemma B.1

(Rules for
\mathcal{P}_{\alpha,\beta}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\gamma}
)
.

1.

For
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
, we have
\mathcal{P}_{\alpha,\alpha}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{\alpha}
.

2.

Let
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
. Let
f_{1},f_{2}:(0,2)\to\mathbb{R}
with
f_{i}\in\mathcal{R}_{\gamma_{i}}
. Then,
f_{1}\cdot f_{2}\in\mathcal{R}_{\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}}(t)
.

3.

If
f_{i}\in\mathcal{P}_{\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}}
,
\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
,
\alpha_{i}\leq\beta_{i}
, then

\displaystyle f_{1}\cdot f_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2},\min\{\alpha_{1}+\beta_{2},\beta_{1}+\alpha_{2}\}}\penalty\ .

4.

Let
A,B>0
,
g_{1}:(0,2)\to(0,2)
,
g_{2}:(0,2)\to\mathbb{R}
. Suppose that
g_{2}\in\mathcal{R}_{\gamma_{2}},\gamma_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
and that
g_{1}(t)=at^{\alpha}+\mathcal{R}_{\gamma_{1}}(t)
,
\gamma_{1}>\alpha>0
and
a>0
. Then,

\displaystyle g_{2}\circ g_{1}

\displaystyle\in

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\alpha\gamma_{2}}\penalty\ .

5.

Let
J\subseteq\mathbb{R}
be an interval, let
g_{1}:(0,2)\to J
, and let
g_{2}\in C^{\infty}(J)
with
0\in J
. Moreover, suppose that
g_{1}(t)=at^{\alpha}+bt^{\beta}+\mathcal{P}_{\alpha,\beta}(t)+\mathcal{R}_{\gamma}(t)
with
\lim_{t\searrow 0}g_{1}(t)=0
,
0\leq\alpha\leq\beta\leq\gamma
,
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
\beta,\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
and
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
. Then,

\displaystyle g_{2}(g_{1}(t))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g_{2}(0)+g_{2}^{\prime}(0)\alpha at^{\alpha}+g_{2}^{\prime}(0)\beta bt^{\beta}+\mathcal{P}_{\alpha,\beta}(t)+\mathcal{R}_{\gamma}(t)\penalty\ .

6.

Let
g_{1}:(0,2)\to(0,\infty)
such that
g_{1}(t)=at^{\alpha}+\mathcal{P}_{\alpha,\alpha}(t)+\mathcal{R}_{\gamma}(t)
for
a,\alpha>0
and
\gamma>\alpha
. Then, for
\delta>0
,

\displaystyle g_{1}(t)^{\delta}=a^{\delta}t^{\alpha\delta}+\mathcal{P}_{\alpha\delta,\alpha\delta}(t)+\mathcal{R}_{\gamma-\alpha+\alpha\delta}(t)\penalty\ .

Proof.

1.

This is straightforward.

2.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle|(f_{1}\cdot f_{2})^{(n)}(t)|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}f_{1}^{(k)}(t)f_{2}^{(n-k)}(t)\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{n}O(t^{\gamma_{1}-k})O(t^{\gamma_{2}-(n-k)})=O(t^{\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}-n})\penalty\ .

3.

Consider integers
\alpha_{i}^{\prime}>\alpha_{i}
and non-integers
\beta_{i}^{\prime}>\beta_{i}
such that
t^{\alpha_{i}^{\prime}}=\mathcal{P}_{\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}}(t)
and
t^{\beta_{i}^{\prime}}=\mathcal{P}_{\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}}(t)
. We now investigate all possible products of such terms:

•

The term
t^{\alpha_{1}^{\prime}+\alpha_{2}^{\prime}}
has integer power
\alpha_{1}^{\prime}+\alpha_{2}^{\prime}>\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}
.

•

The term
t^{\alpha_{1}^{\prime}+\beta_{2}^{\prime}}
has non-integer power
\alpha_{1}^{\prime}+\beta_{2}^{\prime}>\alpha_{1}+\beta_{2}
.

•

The term
t^{\beta_{1}^{\prime}+\alpha_{2}^{\prime}}
has non-integer power
\beta_{1}^{\prime}+\alpha_{2}^{\prime}>\beta_{1}+\alpha_{2}
.

•

The term
t^{\beta_{1}^{\prime}+\beta_{2}^{\prime}}
may have integer or non-integer power. In any case, due to the assumption
\alpha_{i}\leq\beta_{i}
, we have

\displaystyle\beta_{1}^{\prime}+\beta_{2}^{\prime}

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}\geq\min\{\alpha_{1}+\beta_{2},\beta_{1}+\alpha_{2}\}\geq\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}\penalty\ .

4.

Let
g_{2}\in\mathcal{R}_{\gamma_{2}}
. We show

\displaystyle|(g_{2}\circ g_{1})^{(n)}(t)|=O(t^{\gamma_{2}\alpha-n})

by induction on
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Since
0<\alpha<\gamma_{1}
, we have
g_{1}(t)=\Theta(t^{\alpha})
for
t\to 0
and
\lim_{t\to 0}g_{1}(t)=0
. Therefore,

\displaystyle g_{2}(g_{1}(t))=O(g_{1}(t)^{\gamma_{2}})=O(t^{\gamma_{2}\alpha})\penalty\ .

Here, we used
g_{1}(t)=O(t^{\alpha})
in the case
\gamma_{2}>0
and
g_{1}(t)=\Omega(t^{\alpha})
in the case
\gamma_{2}<0
.

For the induction step
n\to n+1
, we use
g_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{\gamma_{2}-1}
to obtain

\displaystyle|(g_{2}\circ g_{1})^{(n+1)}(t)|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\,\mathrm{d}^{n}}{\,\mathrm{d}t^{n}}(g_{2}^{\prime}\circ g_{1})(t)\cdot g_{1}^{\prime}(t)\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}(g_{2}^{\prime}\circ g_{1})^{(k)}(t)g_{1}^{(1+(n-k))}(t)\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{n}O(t^{(\gamma_{2}-1)\alpha-k})O(t^{(\alpha-1-(n-k))})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O(t^{\gamma_{2}\alpha-(n+1)})\penalty\ .

5.

We decompose
g_{2}=p_{2}+r_{2}
, where
p_{2}
is the degree-
(N-1)
Taylor polynomial of
g_{2}
around
0
for some
N\geq 2
to be defined later. Then,

\displaystyle p_{2}(g_{1}(t))=g_{2}(0)+g_{2}^{\prime}(0)g_{1}(t)+\sum_{k=2}^{N-1}\frac{g_{2}^{(k)}(0)}{k!}g_{1}(t)^{k}\penalty\ .

Since we required
\lim_{t\searrow 0}g_{1}(t)=0
,
g_{1}
contains no degree-zero polynomial terms. Therefore, it follows from (a)–(c) that the powers
g_{1}(t)^{k}
for
k\geq 2
satisfy
g_{1}(t)^{k}=\mathcal{P}_{\alpha,\beta}(t)+\mathcal{R}_{\gamma}(t)
, which shows that

\displaystyle p_{2}(g_{1}(t))=g_{2}(0)+g_{2}^{\prime}(0)at^{\alpha}+g_{2}^{\prime}(0)bt^{\beta}+\mathcal{P}_{\alpha,\beta}(t)+\mathcal{R}_{\gamma}(t)\penalty\ .

Since
\lim_{t\searrow 0}g_{1}(t)=0
, we know that
g_{1}\in\mathcal{R}_{\tilde{\beta}}
for some
\tilde{\beta}>0
. For the remainder function
r_{2}
, we could show that
r_{2}\in\mathcal{R}_{N}
but cannot directly apply (d) since it requires a lower bound of the form
g_{1}(t)=\Omega(t^{\tilde{\beta}})
, which does not necessarily hold if
a=b=0
. However, here, the lower bound is not needed: Since we know that
r_{2}\in C^{\infty}
, all derivatives of
r_{2}
are bounded around zero, which is not the case for all
\mathcal{R}_{N}
functions. Hence, here is a variant of the proof of (d) for the current setting:

In order to prove that
r_{2}\circ g_{1}\in\mathcal{R}_{N\tilde{\beta}}
, we want to prove the following statement by induction on
n
:

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, if
h_{2}\in C^{\infty}(J)
and
M\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that
h_{2}(0)=h_{2}^{\prime}(0)=\ldots=h_{2}^{(M-1)}(0)=0
, then
|(h_{2}\circ g_{1})^{(n)}(t)|=O(t^{M\tilde{\beta}-n})
.

By choosing
M\coloneqq N
large enough such that
M\tilde{\beta}\geq\gamma
and setting
h_{2}\coloneqq r_{2}
, we can then conclude that
r_{2}\circ g_{1}\in\mathcal{R}_{\gamma}
.

Base case:
Let
n=0
. By applying Taylor’s theorem, we find that

\displaystyle|h_{2}(g_{1}(t))|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{k=0}^{M}\frac{h_{2}^{(k)}(0)}{k!}g_{1}(t)^{k}+o(|g_{1}(t)|^{M})\right|\leq\left|\frac{h_{2}^{(N)}(0)}{N!}g_{1}(t)^{N}\right|+o(|g_{1}(t)|^{N})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O(|g_{1}(t)|^{N})=O(t^{N\tilde{\beta}}).

Induction step
n\to n+1
:
We apply the induction hypothesis to
\tilde{h}_{2}\coloneqq h_{2}^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}(J)
, which satisfies the derivative condition for
\tilde{M}\coloneqq\max\{0,M-1\}
, and obtain

\displaystyle\left|(h_{2}^{\prime}\circ g_{1})^{(k)}(t)\right|=O(t^{\tilde{N}\tilde{\beta}-k})\leq O(t^{(N-1)\tilde{\beta}-k})

for all
0\leq k\leq n
. Hence,

\displaystyle|(h_{2}\circ g_{1})^{(n+1)}(t)|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\,\mathrm{d}^{n}}{\,\mathrm{d}t^{n}}(h_{2}^{\prime}\circ g_{1})(t)\cdot g_{1}^{\prime}(t)\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}(h_{2}^{\prime}\circ g_{1})^{(k)}(t)\cdot g_{1}^{(1+n-k)}(t)\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{n}O(t^{(M-1)\tilde{\beta}-k})O(t^{\tilde{\beta}-1-n+k})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O(t^{M\tilde{\beta}-(n+1)})\penalty\ ,

which completes the induction step.

6.

We obtain

\displaystyle g_{1}(t)^{\delta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(at^{\alpha}+\mathcal{P}_{\alpha,\alpha}(t)+\mathcal{R}_{\gamma}(t))^{\delta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(at^{\alpha}\cdot(1+t^{-\alpha}\mathcal{P}_{\alpha,\alpha}(t)+t^{-\alpha}\mathcal{R}_{\gamma}(t)))^{\delta}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(b), (c)}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle a^{\delta}t^{\alpha\delta}(1+\mathcal{P}_{0,0}(t)+\mathcal{R}_{\gamma-\alpha}(t))^{\delta}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(e)}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle a^{\delta}t^{\alpha\delta}(1+\mathcal{P}_{0,0}(t)+\mathcal{R}_{\gamma-\alpha}(t))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a^{\delta}t^{\alpha\delta}+\mathcal{P}_{\alpha\delta,\alpha\delta}(t)+\mathcal{R}_{\gamma-\alpha+\alpha\delta}(t)\penalty\ .

∎

We now obtain the following rules for
\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\beta}
, summarized in
Table

B.1
:

Table B.1:
Summary of the boundary computation rules in
Proposition

B.2
.

Rule

Assumptions

\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}}(t)=\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}}(t)

\alpha_{1}\leq\alpha_{2},\beta_{1}\leq\beta_{2}

\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}}(t)+\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}}(t)=\mathcal{Q}_{\min\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\},\min\{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\}}(t)

—

\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}}(t)\cdot\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}}(t)=\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2},\min\{\alpha_{1}+\beta_{2},\alpha_{2}+\beta_{1}\}}(t)

\alpha_{i}\leq\beta_{i}

g_{2}(at^{\alpha}+bt^{\beta}+\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\beta}(t))=g_{2}(0)+g_{2}^{\prime}(0)at^{\alpha}+g_{2}^{\prime}(0)bt^{\beta}+\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\beta}(t)

g_{2}\in C^{\infty}
(also at
0
),

\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
\beta\geq\alpha
,

a=0
if
\alpha=0
,
b=0
if
\beta=0

(at^{\alpha}+\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\alpha}(t))^{\delta}=a^{\delta}t^{\alpha\delta}+\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha\delta,\alpha\delta}(t)

a,\alpha,\delta>0
,
at^{\alpha}+\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\alpha}(t)>0

\mathcal{Q}_{\delta,\delta}(at^{\alpha}+\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\alpha}(t))=\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha\delta,\alpha\delta}(t)

a,\alpha,\delta>0
,
at^{\alpha}+\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\alpha}(t)\in(0,2)

Proposition B.2

(Rules for
\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\beta}
)
.

Let
J\subseteq\mathbb{R}
be an interval. Let
f_{1},f_{2}:[0,2]\to\mathbb{R}
,
g_{1}:[0,2]\to J
and
g_{2}:J\to\mathbb{R}
with

\displaystyle f_{1}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}}(t),

\displaystyle f_{2}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}}(t)

for some
\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
.

1.

Inclusion
: If
\alpha_{1}\leq\alpha_{2}
and
\beta_{1}\leq\beta_{2}
, then
\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}}\subseteq\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}}
.

2.

Sum:
We have

\displaystyle f_{1}(t)+f_{2}(t)=\mathcal{Q}_{\min\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\},\min\{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\}}(t)\penalty\ .

3.

Product:
If
\alpha_{i}\leq\beta_{i}
, we have

\displaystyle f_{1}(t)\cdot f_{2}(t)=\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2},\min\{\alpha_{1}+\beta_{2},\alpha_{2}+\beta_{1}\}}(t)\penalty\ .

4.

Composition with
C^{\infty}
:
Suppose that
g_{1}(t)=at^{\alpha}+bt^{\beta}+\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\beta}(t)
with
g_{1}(0)=0
for some
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\beta\geq\alpha
. Additionally, suppose that
g_{2}\in C^{\infty}(J)
with
0\in J
. Then,

\displaystyle g_{2}(g_{1}(t))=g_{2}(0)+g_{2}^{\prime}(0)at^{\alpha}+g_{2}^{\prime}(0)bt^{\beta}+\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\beta}(t)\penalty\ .

5.

Composition with power:
Suppose that
J\subseteq(0,\infty)
with
g_{1}(t)=at^{\alpha}+\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\alpha}(t)
for
a,\alpha>0
. Then, for
\delta>0
,

\displaystyle g_{1}(t)^{\delta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a^{\delta}t^{\alpha\delta}+\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha\delta,\alpha\delta}(t)\penalty\ .

6.

Composition with
\mathcal{Q}_{\delta,\delta}
:
Suppose that
J=(0,2)
,
g_{1}(t)=at^{\alpha}+\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\alpha}(t)
with
a,\alpha>0
, and
g_{2}(t)=\mathcal{Q}_{\delta,\delta}(t)
with
\delta\geq 0
. Then,

\displaystyle g_{2}(g_{1}(t))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha\delta,\alpha\delta}(t)\penalty\ .

Proof.

1.

Follows from the definition.

2.

For given
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
, write
f_{i}=p_{i}+r_{i}
with
p_{i}\in\mathcal{P}_{\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}}
and
r_{i}\in\mathcal{R}_{\gamma}
. Then, obviously
p_{1}+p_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{\min\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\},\min\{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\}}
and
r_{1}+r_{2}\in\mathcal{R}_{\gamma}
, which shows the claim.

3.

Using a decomposition as in the proof of (b), we write
f_{1}f_{2}=p_{1}p_{2}+(p_{1}r_{2}+r_{1}p_{2}+r_{1}r_{2})
. Here, it follows from
Lemma

B.1
(c) that
p_{1}p_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2},\min\{\alpha_{1}+\beta_{2},\alpha_{2}+\beta_{1}\}}
. Moreover, using
Lemma

B.1
(a) we have
p_{i}\in\mathcal{R}_{\alpha_{i}}
. Using
Lemma

B.1
(b), we obtain

\displaystyle p_{1}r_{2}+r_{1}p_{2}+r_{1}r_{2}\in\mathcal{R}_{\gamma+\min\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\}}\penalty\ ,

where
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
was arbitrary. This shows the claim.

4.

Follows from
Lemma

B.1
(e).

5.

Follows from
Lemma

B.1
(f).

6.

Consider
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
to be chosen later. We can then write
g_{2}(t)=p_{2}(t)+r_{2}(t)
with
p_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{\delta,\delta}
and
r_{2}\in\mathcal{R}_{\gamma}
. Since
p_{2}
is a sum of powers, (a) and (e) show that
p_{2}(g_{1}(t))=\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha\delta,\alpha\delta}(t)
. For
r_{2}
, we use
Lemma

B.1
(a), (d) to conclude that for some
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle r_{2}(g_{1}(t))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r_{2}(at^{\alpha}+\mathcal{R}_{\alpha+\varepsilon}(t))=\mathcal{R}_{\alpha\gamma}(t)\penalty\ .

Since
\gamma
was arbitrary, it can be chosen such that
\alpha\gamma
can be arbitrarily large (since
\alpha>0
). This completes the proof. ∎

Appendix C
Proofs for dual activations

C.1
General properties

Definition C.1

(Pseudo-derivative)
.

Let
f,g:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
. We call
g
a
pseudo-derivative
of
f
if
g
is Lebesgue integrable on compact intervals and

\displaystyle f(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(0)+\int_{0}^{x}g(t)\,\mathrm{d}t

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
.
∎

If
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is continuously differentiable, then
f^{\prime}
is a pseudo-derivative of
f
. As a non-differentiable example, the Heaviside theta function
\mathds{1}_{(0,\infty)}(x)
is a pseudo-derivative of the ReLU function. From basic Lebesgue integration theory, it follows that pseudo-derivatives are unique up to null sets.
Note that any
continuous
activation function
\varphi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
fulfilling
5
is pseudo-differentiable.

The following lemma generalizes the differentiation part of Lemma 11 by
Daniely et al. 2016
to pseudo-differentiable functions.

Lemma C.2

(Properties of pseudo-derivatives)
.

Let
g\in\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{N}(0,1))
be a pseudo-derivative of
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
. Then,

1.

f\in\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{N}(0,1))
,

2.

for
n\geq 1
,
a_{n}(f)=n^{-1/2}a_{n-1}(g)
,

3.

the Hermite expansion
f=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}(f)h_{n}
converges pointwise,

4.

\hat{f}
is differentiable on
[-1,1]
with
\hat{f}^{\prime}=\hat{g}
(cf.
A.3
).

Proof.

Let
\phi
be the p.d.f. of the normal distribution
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. Without loss of generality, let
x\geq 0
. Then,

\displaystyle f(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(0)+\int_{0}^{x}g(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=f(0)+\left\langle\frac{\mathds{1}_{[0,x]}}{\phi},g\right\rangle_{L_{2}(\mathcal{N}(0,1))}

(C.1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(0)+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}(g)\left\langle\frac{\mathds{1}_{[0,x]}}{\phi},h_{n}\right\rangle_{L_{2}(\mathcal{N}(0,1))}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(0)+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}(g)\int_{0}^{x}h_{n}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(0)+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}(g)\int_{0}^{x}(n+1)^{-1/2}h_{n+1}^{\prime}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(0)+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(n+1)^{-1/2}a_{n}(g)[h_{n+1}(x)-h_{n+1}(0)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(0)+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n^{-1/2}a_{n-1}(g)[h_{n}(x)-h_{n}(0)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[f(0)-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n^{-1/2}a_{n-1}(g)h_{n}(0)\right]h_{0}(x)+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n^{-1/2}a_{n-1}(g)h_{n}(x)\penalty\ .

In the last step, we used
h_{0}(x)=1
for all
x
. Moreover, splitting the series in the last step is valid since the extracted series is absolutely summable:

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}|n^{-1/2}a_{n-1}(g)h_{n}(0)|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n^{-1/2}|a_{n-1}(g)|\even(n)\frac{(n-1)!!}{\sqrt{n!}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lemma:double_factorial_asymptotics}}}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}|a_{n-1}(g)|O(n^{-3/4})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\langle(|a_{n-1}(g)|)_{n\geq 1},O(n^{-3/4})\right\rangle_{\ell_{2}(\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1})}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{Cauchy-Schwarz}}}{{<}}

\displaystyle\infty\penalty\ .

Here, we have used that
(a_{n}(g))_{n\geq 1}\in\ell_{2}(\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1})
since
g\in\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{N}(0,1))
.
An analogous argument shows that (
C.1
) also equals
f(x)
for
x<0
. Since
|n^{-1/2}a_{n-1}(g)|\leq|a_{n-1}(g)|
, we know that the series representation (
C.1
) not only converges pointwise but also in
L_{2}(\mathcal{N}(0,1))
, and these limits must be identical. This shows (a), (b) and (c). In order to show (d), we compute

\displaystyle\hat{f}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}(f)^{2}t^{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(b)}}}{{=}}a_{0}(f)^{2}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n^{-1}a_{n-1}(g)^{2}t^{n}

for
t\in[-1,1]
, which implies

\displaystyle\hat{f}^{\prime}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n^{-1}a_{n-1}(g)^{2}nt^{n-1}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}(g)^{2}t^{n}=\hat{g}(t)\penalty\ .

This also holds at the boundary
t\in\{-1,1\}
: Thanks to Abel’s theorem,
\hat{f}
and
\hat{g}
are continuous on
[-1,1]
. Without loss of generality, let
t=1
. The mean value theorem of integration yields
\hat{f}(1)-\hat{f}(1-h)=\int_{1-h}^{1}\hat{f}^{\prime}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=hf^{\prime}(\xi_{h})
for some
\xi_{h}\in(1-h,1)
, thus

\displaystyle\hat{f}^{\prime}(1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{h\searrow 0}\hat{f}^{\prime}(\xi_{h})=\lim_{u\nearrow 1}\hat{f}^{\prime}(u)=\lim_{u\nearrow 1}\hat{g}(u)=\hat{g}(1)\penalty\ .

∎

While the dualization
\varphi\mapsto\hat{\varphi}
from
A.3
is a quadratic and not a linear mapping, it still interchanges nicely with the even-odd decomposition (since even and odd functions are orthogonal w.r.t. the corresponding
“inner product”
):

Proof.

1.

It is easy to see that
\varphi_{\mathrm{even}},\varphi_{\mathrm{odd}}\in\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{N}(0,1))
. As mentioned in
Appendix

A
, the Hermite polynomials are even for even
n
and odd for odd
n
. Hence,

\displaystyle\varphi_{\mathrm{even}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\even(n)a_{n}(\varphi)h_{n}

\displaystyle\varphi_{\mathrm{odd}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\odd(n)a_{n}(\varphi)h_{n}\penalty\ .

We also know that the dual activation satisfies
\hat{\varphi}(t)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}(\varphi)^{2}t^{n}
.
Since the monomials
t^{n}
are also even for even
n
and odd for odd
n
, we obtain

\displaystyle(\hat{\varphi})_{\mathrm{even}}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\even(n)a_{n}(\varphi)^{2}t^{n}=\hbox{\hskip 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern \displaystyle(\hat{\varphi})_{\mathrm{odd}}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\odd(n)a_{n}(\varphi)^{2}t^{n}=\hbox{\hskip (C.3)

Alternatively, this statement can also be proven directly using the definition of the dual activation.

2.

It follows from the Hermite expansions in
Equation

C.3
that

\varphi_{\mathrm{even}}

\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt

and

\varphi_{\mathrm{odd}}

\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt

are nonnegative and increasing on
[0,1]
, and therefore this also holds for

\varphi

\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt

. If
\varphi
is not almost surely constant, we have
a_{n}(\varphi)\neq 0
for some
n\geq 1
. Therefore, the Hermite expansion
\hbox{\hskip 411.22394pt\hskip-3.27083pt\hbox{$\varphi$}\hskip-3.27083pt\hskip-411.22394pt\raisebox{-0.65001pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.11778pt}{\resizebox{48365776}{677201}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94691pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-411.22394pt\hskip 411.22394pt}(t)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}(\varphi)^{2}t^{n}
implies that

\varphi

\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt

is strictly increasing on
[0,1]
.

3.

For
t\in[0,1]
, the statement follows from (b). For

, (b) implies

\displaystyle|\hbox{\hskip 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94691pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94725pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.97221pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-411.22394pt\hskip 411.22394pt}(-1)=0
or
\hbox{\hskip 411.22394pt\hskip-10.03822pt\hbox{$\varphi_{\mathrm{odd}}$}\hskip-10.03822pt\hskip-411.22394pt\raisebox{-0.65001pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.11778pt}{\resizebox{48365776}{677201}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94691pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern
