0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-411.22394pt\hskip 411.22394pt}(-1)=0
, which is the case iff
\varphi
is almost surely even or almost surely odd.

4.

By
Eq.

A.2
the dual activation

\varphi

\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt

is a convergent power series on
[-1,1]
.
∎

C.2
Dominating terms

Definition C.4

(Double factorial)
.

Following
Daniely et al. 2016
, we define the double factorial for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
as

\displaystyle n!!

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle\begin{cases}1&,n\leq 0\\
n\cdot(n-2)\cdots 4\cdot 2&,n>0\text{ even}\\
n\cdot(n-2)\cdots 3\cdot 1&,n>0\text{ odd}.\end{cases}

∎

Proposition C.5
.

For all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, the reference activation
s_{k}
from
A.5
satisfies
s_{k}\in L_{2}(\mathcal{N}(0,1))
and
s_{k}
is a pseudo-derivative of
s_{k+1}
. Moreover, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, the Hermite coefficients satisfy

\displaystyle a_{n}(s_{k})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\odd(n-k)\frac{(-1)^{\frac{\max(1,n-k)-1}{2}}(n-k-2)!!}{(k-n)!!\sqrt{2\pi n!}}

Proof.

The first two statements are straightforward to show, hence we only show the formula for the Hermite coefficients. We first note that for
X\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, we have for odd
k
the central absolute moments following
(
Winkelbauer 2012
, see e.g.)
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|X|^{k}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{k/2}\Gamma\left(\frac{k+1}{2}\right)\pi^{-1/2}=2^{k/2}((k-1)/2)!\pi^{-1/2}=2^{1/2}(k-1)!!\pi^{-1/2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}(k-1)!!\penalty\ .

This allows us to show the statement for
n=0
:

\displaystyle a_{0}(s_{k})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle h_{0},s_{k}\rangle_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{N}(0,1))}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}s_{k}(x)\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-x^{2}/2}\,\mathrm{d}x=\odd(k)\frac{1}{2k!}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|x^{k}|\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-x^{2}/2}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\odd(k)\frac{(k-1)!!}{k!\sqrt{2\pi}}=\odd(k)\frac{1}{k!!\sqrt{2\pi}}=\odd(0-k)\frac{(-1)^{\frac{\max(1,0-k)-1}{2}}(0-k-2)!!}{(k-0)!!\sqrt{2\pi 0!}}\penalty\ ,

Finally, we show the formula for all
n,k
via induction on
k
. (We do not use induction on
n
.) For the base case
k=0
, we note that
h_{0}\equiv 1
and
s_{0}=2^{-1/2}\sqrt{2}\mathds{1}_{(0,\infty)}-\frac{1}{2}h_{0}
almost everywhere.
Daniely et al. 2016
show in Section 8 that

\displaystyle a_{n}(\sqrt{2}\mathds{1}_{(0,\infty)})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}2^{-1/2}&,n=0\\
\frac{(-1)^{\frac{n-1}{2}}(n-2)!!}{\sqrt{\pi n!}}&,n\text{ odd}\\
0&,2\leq n\text{ even.}\end{cases}

Therefore, we obtain for
n\geq 1
:

\displaystyle a_{n}(s_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{-1/2}a_{n}(\sqrt{2}\mathds{1}_{(0,\infty)})=\odd(n)\frac{(-1)^{\frac{n-1}{2}}(n-2)!!}{\sqrt{2\pi n!}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\odd(n-0)\frac{(-1)^{\frac{\max(1,n-0)-1}{2}}(n-0-2)!!}{(0-n)!!\sqrt{2\pi n!}}\penalty\ ,

which completes the base case
k=0
since the case
n=0
has already been treated above.

For the induction
k\to k+1
, we note that
s_{k}
is a pseudo-derivative of
s_{k+1}
and use
Lemma

C.2
to obtain for all
n\geq 0
:

\displaystyle a_{n+1}(s_{k+1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(n+1)^{-1/2}a_{n}(s_{k})=\odd(n-k)\frac{(-1)^{\frac{\max(1,n-k)-1}{2}}(n-k-2)!!}{(k-n)!!\sqrt{2\pi(n+1)!}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\odd((n+1)-(k+1))\frac{(-1)^{\frac{\max(1,(n+1)-(k+1))-1}{2}}((n+1)-(k+1)-2)!!}{((k+1)-(n+1))!!\sqrt{2\pi(n+1)!}}\penalty\ ,

which completes the induction.
∎

Lemma C.6
.

We have
\hbox{\hskip 401.59273pt\hskip-4.5868pt\hbox{$s_{0}$}\hskip-4.5868pt\hskip-401.59273pt\raisebox{-0.65001pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.11778pt}{\resizebox{47060480}{677201}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 401.59273pt}(t)=\frac{1}{4}-\frac{1}{2\pi}\arccos(t)
.

Proof.

By Section 8 in
Daniely et al. 2016
, the function
f\coloneqq\sqrt{2}\mathds{1}_{(0,\infty)}
has the dual activation
\hat{f}(t)=1-\frac{1}{\pi}\arccos(t)
. Hence,
g\coloneqq\mathds{1}_{(0,\infty)}
has the dual activation

\displaystyle\hat{g}(t)=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2\pi}\arccos(t)\penalty\ .

Now, since
s_{0}=g-1/2
almost everywhere, we have
a_{n}(s_{0})=a_{n}(g)
for all
n\neq 0
, and hence

\displaystyle\hbox{\hskip 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94691pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern . Since

.
∎

Lemma C.7
.

For all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, there exists
b_{k}>0
such that for
\tau\in\{\pm 1\}
,

\displaystyle\hbox{\hskip 401.59273pt\hskip-4.80383pt\hbox{$s_{k}$}\hskip-4.80383pt\hskip-401.59273pt\raisebox{-0.65001pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.11778pt}{\resizebox{47060480}{677201}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94691pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94725pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.97221pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-401.59273pt\hskip 401.59273pt}(\tau(1-t))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-\tau)^{k+1}b_{k}t^{k+1/2}+\mathcal{Q}_{-1,k+1/2}(t)\penalty\ .

Proof.

Step 1: Analysis of
s_{0}
.
First, consider the case
\tau=1
.
We have

\displaystyle\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\arccos(1-t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-(1-t)^{2}}}=t^{-1/2}(2-t)^{-1/2}\penalty\ .

It is easily seen using induction that

\displaystyle\frac{\,\mathrm{d}^{n}}{\,\mathrm{d}t^{n}}(2-t)^{-1/2}=\frac{(2n-1)!!}{2^{n}}(2-t)^{-\frac{2n+1}{2}}\penalty\ .

Since
t\mapsto(2-t)^{-1/2}
is analytic in a neighborhood of
t=0
, it is equal to its Taylor expansion:

\displaystyle(2-t)^{-1/2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(2n-1)!!}{2^{2n+\frac{1}{2}}n!}t^{n}\penalty\ .

Since all coefficients are positive, we can apply the monotone convergence theorem to obtain

\displaystyle\arccos(1-t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\arccos(1-0)+\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}u}\arccos(1-u)\,\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(2n-1)!!}{2^{2n+\frac{1}{2}}n!}u^{n-1/2}\,\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{(2n-1)!!}{2^{2n+\frac{1}{2}}n!}u^{n-1/2}\,\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(2n-1)!!}{2^{2n+\frac{1}{2}}n!}\left[\frac{1}{n+1/2}u^{n+1/2}\right]_{0}^{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(2n-1)!!}{2^{2n+\frac{1}{2}}n!(n+1/2)}t^{n+1/2}\penalty\ .

For arbitrary
1\leq N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, this yields using
Lemma

C.6
, the identity

\displaystyle\hbox{\hskip 411.22394pt\hskip-4.5868pt\hbox{$s_{0}$}\hskip-4.5868pt\hskip-411.22394pt\raisebox{-0.65001pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.11778pt}{\resizebox{48365776}{677201}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94691pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern \displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}-\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(2n-1)!!}{2^{2n+\frac{3}{2}}n!(n+1/2)\pi}t^{n+1/2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}-\frac{1}{\pi\sqrt{2}}t^{1/2}+\underbrace{\sum_{n=1}^{N-1}\frac{(2n-1)!!}{2^{2n+\frac{3}{2}}n!(n+1/2)\pi}t^{n+1/2}}_{=\mathcal{P}_{0,1/2}(t)}

\displaystyle\penalty\ +\penalty\ \underbrace{t^{N+1/2}}_{=\mathcal{R}_{N+1/2}(t)}\underbrace{\sum_{n=N}^{\infty}\frac{(2n-1)!!}{2^{2n+\frac{3}{2}}n!(n+1/2)\pi}t^{n-N}}_{=\mathcal{R}_{0}(t)}.

. This shows the claim for
\tau=1
. For

, we note that

. By

, the same holds for

\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt

. This shows

\displaystyle\hbox{\hskip \displaystyle=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle b_{k}t^{k+1/2}+\mathcal{P}_{-1,k+1/2}(t)+\mathcal{R}_{\gamma}(t)\penalty\ .

∎

We will also need the asymptotic decay of
a_{n}(s_{k})
, which will be studied in the following two lemmas.

Lemma C.8
.

For
p\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and odd
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{m!!}{\sqrt{(m+p)!}}=\Theta_{\forall m}(m^{1/4-p/2})\penalty\ .

Proof.

For even
n=2k\geq 2
, we have
n!!=n(n-2)\cdots 2=(2\cdot k)(2\cdot(k-1))\cdots(2\cdot 1)=2^{k}k!=2^{n/2}(n/2)!
. Using Stirling’s formula, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{(n-1)!!}{\sqrt{n!}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{n!}{n!!\sqrt{n!}}=\frac{\sqrt{n!}}{2^{n/2}(n/2)!}\sim\frac{(2\pi n)^{1/4}n^{n/2}e^{-n/2}}{2^{n/2}(\pi n)^{1/2}(n/2)^{n/2}e^{-n/2}}=\Theta_{\forall n}(n^{-1/4})\penalty\ .

By setting
n\coloneqq m+1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{m!!}{\sqrt{(m+p)!}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Theta_{\forall m}(m^{1/2-p/2})\frac{m!!}{\sqrt{(m+1)!}}=\Theta_{\forall m}(m^{1/2-p/2})\Theta_{\forall m}(m^{-1/4})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Theta_{\forall m}(m^{1/4-p/2})\penalty\ .

∎

Lemma C.9
.

We have
|a_{n}(s_{k})|=\Theta_{\forall n}(\odd(n-k)(n+1)^{-3/4-k/2})
.

Proof.

Since
(k-n)!!=1
for
n\geq k
by our definition of the double factorial in
C.4
, we obtain

\displaystyle|a_{n}(s_{k})|

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{prop:special_hermite_coefficients}}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\Theta_{\forall n}\left(\odd(n-k)\frac{(n-k-2)!!}{\sqrt{2\pi n!}}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lemma:double_factorial_asymptotics}}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\Theta_{\forall n}(\odd(n-k)(n+1)^{-3/4-k/2})\penalty\ .

∎

C.3
Mix terms

Now, we want to investigate some of the mix-terms arising in the decomposition in Eq. (
A.3
). For convenience, we will give them a new name:

Definition C.10
.

For
i,j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, define
f_{i,j}:[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle f_{i,j}(t)\coloneqq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}(s_{i})a_{n}(s_{j})t^{n}\penalty\ .

Note that
f_{i,j}\equiv 0
for odd
i-j
and
f_{i,j}=f_{j,i}
. Moreover,
f_{i,i}=\hbox{\hskip 411.22394pt\hskip-4.00839pt\hbox{$s_{i}$}\hskip-4.00839pt\hskip-411.22394pt\raisebox{-0.65001pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.11778pt}{\resizebox{48365776}{677201}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94691pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94725pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.97221pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-411.22394pt\hskip 411.22394pt}
.
∎

Lemma C.11

(Recursive characterization of mix-terms)
.

Let
k,l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Then, there exists a polynomial
p_{k,k+2l}
such that for
t\in[-1,1]
,

\displaystyle f_{k,k+2l}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(k+2)f_{k+2,(k+2)+2(l-1)}(t)-tf_{k+1,(k+1)+2(l-1)}(t)+p_{k,k+2l}(t)

\displaystyle f_{k,k+2l+1}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\penalty\ .

Proof.

Since
a_{n}(s_{k})=0
for even
n-k
and
a_{n}(s_{k+2l+1})=0
for even
n-(k+2l+1)
, we have

\displaystyle f_{k,k+2l+1}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}(s_{k})a_{n}(s_{k+2l+1})t^{n}=0\penalty\ .

For the other formula, we use
Proposition

C.5
and obtain

\displaystyle a_{n}(s_{k})a_{n}(s_{k+2l})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\odd(n-k)\odd(n-(k+2l))(-1)^{\frac{\max(1,n-k)-1}{2}+\frac{\max(1,n-k-2l)-1}{2}}

\displaystyle\penalty\ \cdot\penalty\ \frac{(n-k-2)!!(n-k-2l-2)!!}{(k-n)!!(k+2l-n)!!2\pi n!}\penalty\ .

Here,
\odd(n-k)\odd(n-(k+2l))=\odd(n-k)
. Moreover, for odd
n-k
and
n\geq k+2l
, we have

\displaystyle(-1)^{\frac{\max(1,n-k)-1}{2}+\frac{\max(1,n-k-2l)-1}{2}}=(-1)^{n-k-l-1}=(-1)^{-l}=(-1)^{l}\penalty\ .

In the following, we write

\displaystyle f\simeq_{\mathrm{pol}}g

if
f-g
is a polynomial.

The terms
(k-n)!!
and
(k+2l-n)!!
are equal to one for
n\geq k+2l
. Therefore, we obtain for all but finitely many
n

\displaystyle a_{n}(s_{k})a_{n}(s_{k+2l})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\odd(n-k)(-1)^{l}\frac{(n-k-2)!!(n-k-2l-2)!!}{2\pi n!}\penalty\ .

By writing
(n-k-2)!!=-(k+2)(n-k-4)!!+n(n-k-4)!!
, we obtain:

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}(s_{k})a_{n}(s_{k+2l})t^{n}

\displaystyle\simeq_{\mathrm{pol}}

\displaystyle-(k+2)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\odd(n-k)(-1)^{l}\frac{(n-k-4)!!(n-k-2l-2)!!}{2\pi n!}t^{n}

\displaystyle\penalty\ +\penalty\ \sum_{n=0}^{\infty}n\odd(n-k)(-1)^{l}\frac{(n-k-4)!!(n-k-2l-2)!!}{2\pi n!}t^{n}

\displaystyle\simeq_{\mathrm{pol}}

\displaystyle(k+2)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\odd(n-(k+2))(-1)^{l-1}\frac{(n-(k+2)-2)!!(n-(k+2)-2(l-1)-2)!!}{2\pi n!}t^{n}

\displaystyle\penalty\ -\penalty\ t\cdot\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\odd((n-1)-(k-1))(-1)^{l-1}\frac{((n-1)-k-3)!!((n-1)-k-2l-1)!!}{2\pi(n-1)!}t^{n-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(k+2)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\odd(n-(k+2))(-1)^{l-1}\frac{(n-(k+2)-2)!!(n-(k+2)-2(l-1)-2)!!}{2\pi n!}t^{n}

\displaystyle\penalty\ -\penalty\ t\cdot\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\odd(n-(k+1))(-1)^{l-1}\frac{(n-(k+1)-2)!!(n-(k+1)-2(l-1)-2)!!}{2\pi n!}t^{n}

\displaystyle\simeq_{\mathrm{pol}}

\displaystyle(k+2)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}(s_{k+2})a_{n}(s_{(k+2)+2(l-1)})t^{n}-t\cdot\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}(s_{k+1})a_{n}(s_{(k+1)+2(l-1)})t^{n}\penalty\ ,

which completes the proof.
∎

Proposition C.12
.

Let
i,j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Then, for any
\tau\in\{\pm 1\}
and
t\in(0,2)
,

\displaystyle f_{i,j}(\tau(1-t))=\mathcal{Q}_{-1,(i+j)/2}(t)\penalty\ .

Proof.

Recall
Lemma

C.11
.
Since
f_{i,j}=f_{j,i}
, we can assume
i\leq j
without loss of generality. Moreover, for odd
j-i
, the statement is trivial since
f_{i,j}\equiv 0
. For the remaining cases where
j-i\geq 0
is even, it suffices to prove the following statement by induction on
l
:

For all
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
: For all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
:

\displaystyle f_{k,k+2l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{-1,k+l}(t)\penalty\ .

For
l=0
, this follows from
Lemma

C.7
since
f_{k,k}=\hbox{\hskip 411.22394pt\hskip-4.80383pt\hbox{$s_{k}$}\hskip-4.80383pt\hskip-411.22394pt\raisebox{-0.65001pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.11778pt}{\resizebox{48365776}{677201}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94691pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94725pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.97221pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-411.22394pt\hskip 411.22394pt}
. For the induction step, we use
Lemma

C.11
to obtain for all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
:

\displaystyle f_{k,k+2l}(\tau(1-t))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{-1,\infty}(t)+(k+2)f_{k+2,k+2+2(l-1)}(\tau(1-t))-\tau(1-t)f_{k+1,k+1+2(l-1)}(\tau(1-t))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{-1,\infty}(t)+\mathcal{Q}_{-1,k+l+1}(t)-\tau(1-t)(\mathcal{Q}_{-1,k+l}(t))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{-1,k+l}(t)\penalty\ .

Here, we used that for a polynomial
p
, the map
t\mapsto p(\tau(1-t))
is also a polynomial and thus in
\mathcal{Q}_{-1,\infty}(t)
.
∎

C.4
Results for smooth terms

In the following, we analyze the decay of Hermite coefficients for smooth functions, which can then be used to establish the smoothness of certain components of dual activations. For smooth
f
, we could show the smoothness of

f

by using

, but this approach does not work directly for mix-terms, and hence the intermediate step via coefficient decay is helpful.

Lemma C.13
.

If
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
has a
m
-fold pseudo-derivative
f^{(m)}
with
f^{(m)}\in\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{N}(0,1))
, then
|a_{n}(f)|<o_{m}((n+1)^{-m/2})
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

C.2
, we obtain for
n\geq m
:

\displaystyle a_{n}(f)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[n(n-1)\cdot\ldots\cdot(n-m+1)\right]^{-1/2}a_{n-m}(f^{(m)})\penalty\ .

Since
a_{n-m}(f^{(m)})<o_{\forall n}(1)
, we obtain
|a_{n}(f)|<o_{\forall n}((n+1)^{-m/2})
.
∎

Lemma C.14

(Differentiability of power series)
.

Let
f:[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R},x\mapsto\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}b_{n}x^{n}
with
|b_{n}|=O_{\forall n}((n+1)^{-(k+1+\varepsilon)})
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\varepsilon>0
. Then,
f\in C^{k}([-1,1])
.

Proof.

We prove by induction on
k
that
f\in C^{k}([-1,1])
and

\displaystyle f^{(k)}(t)=\sum_{n=k}^{\infty}n\cdot\ldots\cdot(n-k+1)b_{n}t^{n-k}

for
t\in[-1,1]
. For
k=0
, we have
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}|b_{n}|<\infty
and the result follows from Abel’s theorem on power series. Now, let the statement hold for
k-1\geq 0
. We know from the case
k=0
that

\displaystyle g:[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R},t\mapsto\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}nb_{n}t^{n-1}

as well as
f
are continuous. By elementary analysis,
f^{\prime}=g
on
(-1,1)
. Moreover,

\displaystyle f(1)-f(1-h)=\lim_{h^{\prime}\searrow 0}f(1-h^{\prime})-f(1-h)=\lim_{h^{\prime}\searrow 0}\int_{1-h}^{1-h^{\prime}}g(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{1-h}^{1}g(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=hg(\xi_{h})

for a suitable
\xi_{h}\in[1-h,1]
by the mean value theorem of integration. Since
g
is continuous, it follows that
f
is differentiable in
1
with
f^{\prime}(1)=g(1)
. An analogous calculation can be applied for
t=-1
. By applying the induction hypothesis to
g=f^{\prime}
, the proof is completed.
∎

C.5
General activation functions

Now, we want to obtain the asymptotic boundary behavior in the sense of
Appendix

B
for general activation functions
\varphi
as in
5
. To this end, we use a decomposition into reference functions and smooth remainders:

Lemma C.15
.

Let
\varphi
be an activation function as in
5
and let
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
Then, using
\Delta_{k}(\varphi)
as defined in
A.5
,

\displaystyle\varphi_{m}\coloneqq\varphi-\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\Delta_{k}(\varphi)s_{k}

is
m
times pseudo-differentiable and the
m
-fold pseudo-derivative
\varphi_{m}^{(m)}
is in
L_{2}(\mathcal{N}(0,1))
.

Proof.

Note that
\Delta_{m}
is a linear operator with
\Delta_{m}(s_{k})=\delta_{mk}
. Hence, for
l\leq m-1
, we have

\displaystyle\Delta_{l}(\varphi_{m})=\Delta_{l}(\varphi)-\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\Delta_{k}(\varphi)\Delta_{l}(s_{k})=\Delta_{l}(\varphi)-\Delta_{l}(\varphi)=0\penalty\ .

We now prove by induction on
l
with
0\leq l\leq m
that
g_{l}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

\displaystyle g_{0}\coloneqq\varphi_{m},g_{l}(x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\varphi_{m}^{(l)}(x)&,x\neq 0\\
\varphi_{m}^{(l)}(0-)&,x=0\end{cases}\quad(l\geq 1)

is a
l
-fold pseudo-derivative of
\varphi_{m}
. For
l=0
, we have
g_{0}=\varphi_{m}
by definition, hence
g_{0}
is a
0
-fold pseudo-derivative of
\varphi_{m}
. For the induction step
l\to l+1
, we observe that
l\leq m-1
, hence
\Delta_{l}(\varphi_{m})=0
, which implies
\varphi_{m}^{(l)}(0-)=\varphi_{m}^{(l)}(0+)
and therefore,
g_{l}
is continuous. Moreover,
g_{l}
is continuously differentiable on
(0,x)
and
(-x,0)
, and
g_{l}^{\prime}
is bounded on these intervals. Thus,

\displaystyle g_{l}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g_{l}(0)+\int_{0}^{x}g_{l}^{\prime}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=g_{l}(0)+\int_{0}^{x}g_{l+1}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle g_{l}(-x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g_{l}(0)+\int_{0}^{-x}g_{l}^{\prime}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=g_{l}(0)+\int_{0}^{-x}g_{l+1}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\penalty\ ,

which shows that
g_{l+1}
is a pseudo-derivative of
g_{l}
.

It remains to show that
g_{m}\in L_{2}(\mathcal{N}(0,1))
. For
x\neq 0
, we have
s_{k}^{(m)}(x)=0
for
k<m
and therefore
g_{m}(x)=\varphi^{(m)}(x)
. By
5
,
\varphi|_{(0,\infty)}\in\mathcal{S}^{\infty}((0,\infty))
and
\varphi|_{(-\infty,0)}\in\mathcal{S}^{\infty}((-\infty,0))
, which implies the desired integrability for
\varphi^{(m)}
, hence
g_{m}\in L_{2}(\mathcal{N}(0,1))
.
∎

See
A.6

Proof.

Step 0: Proof strategy
Fix
\tau\in\{-1,1\}
. For an integer
M_{1}>m
to be defined later, we decompose
\varphi
using
Lemma

C.15
as

\displaystyle\varphi=g_{1}+g_{2},\qquad g_{1}\coloneqq\sum_{k=m}^{M_{1}-1}\Delta_{k}(\varphi)s_{k},\qquad g_{2}\coloneqq\varphi_{M_{1}}\penalty\ .

Here,
Lemma

C.15
tells us that
g_{2}
is
M_{1}
times pseudo-differentiable and
g_{2}^{(M_{1})}\in L_{2}(\mathcal{N}(0,1))
. The basic strategy is as follows: To verify
f(t)=\mathcal{Q}_{-1,m+1/2}(t)
for a suitable function, we need to show that for all
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
, there exists a decomposition
f=q+r
with
q\in\mathcal{P}_{-1,m+1/2}
and
r\in\mathcal{R}_{\gamma}
. We will select
M_{1}
depending on
\gamma
to construct such a decomposition. To show
r\in\mathcal{R}_{\gamma}
, we need to show
|r^{(N)}(t)|=O(t^{\gamma-N})
for all
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. To this end, we will decompose
\varphi
again with an order
M_{2}>M_{1}
that depends on
N
(but does not change
r
).

Step 1: Defining the decomposition of

\varphi

\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt

.
Using
C.10
, we obtain

\displaystyle\hbox{\hskip 411.22394pt\hskip-4.8073pt\hbox{$g_{1}$}\hskip-4.8073pt\hskip-411.22394pt\raisebox{-0.65001pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.11778pt}{\resizebox{48365776}{677201}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94691pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94725pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.97221pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-411.22394pt\hskip 411.22394pt}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(\sum_{k=m}^{M_{1}-1}\Delta_{k}(\varphi)a_{n}(s_{k})\right)^{2}t^{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k_{1}=m}^{M_{1}-1}\sum_{k_{2}=m}^{M_{1}-1}\Delta_{k_{1}}(\varphi)\Delta_{k_{2}}(\varphi)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}(s_{k_{1}})a_{n}(s_{k_{2}})t^{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k_{1}=m}^{M_{1}-1}\sum_{k_{2}=m}^{M_{1}-1}\Delta_{k_{1}}(\varphi)\Delta_{k_{2}}(\varphi)f_{k_{1},k_{2}}(t).

For
k_{1}=k_{2}=m
, we have

\displaystyle f_{m,m}(\tau(1-t))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hbox{\hskip 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94691pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94725pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.97221pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-411.22394pt\hskip 411.22394pt}(\tau(1-t))+p_{\tau}(t)+r_{\tau}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Delta_{m}(\varphi)^{2}\tau^{m+1}(-1)^{m+1}b_{m}t^{m+1/2}+\mathcal{P}_{-1,m+1/2}(t)+\mathcal{R}_{\gamma}(t)

for arbitrary
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
.

Step 2: Analyzing the rest term.
Let
\tau\in\{-1,1\}
and
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
be arbitrary. We need to show that
|r_{\tau}^{(N)}(t)|=O(t^{\gamma-N})
, where
r_{\tau}
is defined in Eq. (
C.5
). For an integer
M_{2}>M_{1}
yet to be specified, we use
Lemma

C.15
again to decompose

\displaystyle g_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varphi_{M_{2}}+\sum_{k=M_{1}}^{M_{2}-1}\Delta_{k}(\varphi)s_{k}\penalty\ .

With the index set

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}\coloneqq\{m,m+1,\ldots,M_{2}-1\}^{2}\setminus\{m,m+1,\ldots,M_{1}-1\}^{2}\penalty\ ,

we then obtain similar to the calculation above

\displaystyle h(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h_{1}(t)+h_{2}(t)

\displaystyle h_{1}(t)

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle\sum_{(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathcal{I}}\Delta_{k_{1}}(\varphi)\Delta_{k_{2}}(\varphi)f_{k_{1},k_{2}}(t)

\displaystyle h_{2}(t)

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}(\varphi_{M_{2}})\left(a_{n}(\varphi_{M_{2}})+2\sum_{k=m}^{M_{2}-1}\Delta_{k}(\varphi)a_{n}(s_{k})\right)t^{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}o((n+1)^{-M_{2}/2})O((n+1)^{-3/4-m/2})t^{n}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}o((n+1)^{-3/4-m/2-M_{2}/2})t^{n}\penalty\ .

We now choose
M_{2}
sufficiently large such that
3/4+m/2+M_{2}/2>\tilde{N}+1
, where
\tilde{N}\coloneqq\max\{N,\lceil\gamma\rceil\}
.
Lemma

C.14
yields

\displaystyle h_{2}\in C^{\tilde{N}}([-1,1])\penalty\ .

Since (
C.4
) implies
(m+M_{1})/2\geq\gamma
, we obtain from
Proposition

C.12

\displaystyle h_{1}(\tau(1-t))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{-1,(m+M_{1})/2}(t)+\mathcal{R}_{\gamma}(t)=\mathcal{P}_{-1,\infty}(t)+\mathcal{R}_{\gamma}(t)\penalty\ .

In other words, we can find a polynomial
p_{1,\tau}
of degree
\leq\lceil\gamma\rceil-1
and a function
r_{1,\tau}\in\mathcal{R}_{\gamma}
such that

\displaystyle h_{1}(\tau(1-t))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p_{1,\tau}(t)+r_{1,\tau}(t)\penalty\ .

We therefore investigate

\displaystyle r_{2,\tau}(t)

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle r_{\tau}(t)-r_{1,\tau}(t)=(h(\tau(1-t))-p_{\tau}(t))-(h_{1}(\tau(1-t))-p_{1,\tau}(t))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h_{2}(\tau(1-t))-p_{\tau}(t)+p_{1,\tau}(t)\penalty\ ,

which satisfies
r_{2,\tau}\in C^{\tilde{N}}([-1,1])

We now distinguish two cases:

•

If
N\geq\gamma
, we simply use the continuity of
r_{2,\tau}^{(N)}
in
0
to obtain

\displaystyle|r_{2,\tau}^{(N)}(t)|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O(1)=O(t^{\gamma-N})\penalty\ .

•

If
N<\gamma
, we proceed differently. For
0\leq n\leq\lceil\gamma\rceil-1
, we have

\displaystyle r_{2,\tau}^{(n)}(0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r_{2,\tau}^{(n)}(0+)=r_{\tau}^{(n)}(0+)-r_{1,\tau}^{(n)}(0+)=0-0=0\penalty\ ,

where we used that
r_{\tau}^{(n)}(0+)=0
by construction and that
r_{1,\tau}^{(n)}(0+)=0
thanks to
r_{1,\tau}\in\mathcal{R}_{\gamma}
and
n<\gamma
.
Thus, Taylor’s theorem with Peano’s form of the remainder yields

\displaystyle|r_{2,\tau}^{(N)}(t)|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{k=0}^{\lceil\gamma\rceil-N}\frac{r_{2,\tau}^{(N+k)}(0)}{k!}t^{k}+o(t^{\lceil\gamma\rceil-N})\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O(t^{\lceil\gamma\rceil-N})=O(t^{\gamma-N})\penalty\ .

Since
r_{1,\tau}\in\mathcal{R}_{\gamma}
, we obtain

\displaystyle|r_{\tau}^{(N)}(t)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|r_{1,\tau}^{(N)}(t)|+|r_{2,\tau}^{(N)}(t)|\leq O(t^{\gamma-N})+O(t^{\gamma-N})=O(t^{\gamma-N})\penalty\ ,

which completes the proof.
∎

Appendix D
Neural kernel proofs

D.1
Neural kernels

Recall the network’s architecture in
1
.

For the subsequent consideration concerning kernels, we compare the network behavior for two inputs
\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}
. All terms
\bullet
correspond to the inputs
\boldsymbol{x}
, all terms
\bar{\bullet}
to the input
\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}
.

Definition D.1

(Neural kernels)
.

Consider a network of depth
L\geq 2
and output dimension
d_{L}=1
. Let
d=d_{1}=\dots=d_{L-1}
.
Define the
neural network Gaussian process
-kernel
k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L}:\mathbb{R}^{d_{0}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{0}}\to\mathbb{R}
as

\displaystyle k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})\coloneqq\lim_{d\to\infty}\Cov\big(\boldsymbol{z}^{(L)},\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(L)}\big)

and the neural tangent kernel
k^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{L}:\mathbb{R}^{d_{0}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{0}}\to\mathbb{R}
as

\displaystyle k^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{L}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})\coloneqq\lim_{d\to\infty}\left\langle\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\boldsymbol{z}^{(L)},\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}{\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(L)}}\right\rangle\penalty\ ,

where
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\boldsymbol{z}^{(L)}
denotes derivation of the output
\boldsymbol{z}^{(L)}
by all parameters
\boldsymbol{\theta}
.
∎

Lemma D.2
.

Let the activation function
\varphi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
fulfill
5
.
Then, the NNGP and NTK kernels introduced above converge almost surely and they are given by

\displaystyle k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})\coloneqq

\displaystyle\,\sigma_{b}^{2}\sigma_{i}^{2}+\sigma_{w}^{2}\langle\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle

\displaystyle k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})=

\displaystyle\,\sigma_{b}^{2}\sigma_{i}^{2}+\sigma_{w}^{2}\mathbb{E}_{(u,v)\sim\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{L-1}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})}\varphi(u)\varphi(v)

\displaystyle k^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{L}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})=

\displaystyle\,\sigma_{b}^{2}(1-\sigma_{i}^{2})+k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})+\sigma_{w}^{2}\mathbb{E}_{(u,v)\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{L-1}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}))}\left[\varphi^{\prime}(u)\varphi^{\prime}(v)\right]k^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{L-1}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{L}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})=

\displaystyle\,\begin{pmatrix}k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x})&k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})\\
k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{x})&k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})\end{pmatrix}

where we define

k^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})\coloneqq\sigma_{b}^{2}(1-\sigma_{i}^{2})+k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})
.

Proof.

Denote
\tilde{d}_{l}\coloneqq d
if
l\in\{1,\dots,L-1\}
and
\tilde{d}_{l}=1
otherwise. Consider the inputs
\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{0}}
.
For a fixed hidden layer width
d\in\mathbb{N}
, the finite version of the NTK kernel is given by

\displaystyle\left\langle\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\boldsymbol{z}^{(L)},\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(L)}\right\rangle=\sum_{l=1}^{L}\left\langle\nabla_{\boldsymbol{W}^{(l)}}\boldsymbol{z}^{(L)},\nabla_{\boldsymbol{W}^{(l)}}{\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(L)}}\right\rangle+\sum_{l=1}^{L}\left\langle\nabla_{\boldsymbol{b}^{(l)}}\boldsymbol{z}^{(L)},\nabla_{{\boldsymbol{b}}^{(l)}}\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(L)}\right\rangle\,,

\displaystyle\nabla_{\boldsymbol{b}^{(l)}}\boldsymbol{z}^{(L)}=\sigma_{b}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{z}^{(l)}}\boldsymbol{z}^{(L)}\,,

(D.1)

\displaystyle\left\langle\nabla_{\boldsymbol{W}^{(l)}}\boldsymbol{z}^{(L)},\nabla_{\boldsymbol{W}^{(l)}}{\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(L)}}\right\rangle=\frac{\sigma_{w}^{2}}{{\tilde{d}_{l-1}}}\left\langle\nabla_{\boldsymbol{z}^{(l)}}\boldsymbol{z}^{(L)}{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(l-1)\top},\nabla_{\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(l)}}\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(L)}\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(l-1)\top}\right\rangle,

where inner products involving matrices are Frobenius inner products. Using the outer product structure we can rewrite the weight-matrix gradient as

\displaystyle\left\langle\nabla_{\boldsymbol{W}^{(l)}}\boldsymbol{z}^{(L)},\nabla_{\boldsymbol{W}^{(l)}}{\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(L)}}\right\rangle

(D.2)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\sigma_{w}^{2}\left\langle\nabla_{\boldsymbol{z}^{(l)}}\boldsymbol{z}^{(L)},\nabla_{\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(l)}}\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(L)}\right\rangle\frac{1}{{\tilde{d}_{l-1}}}\left\langle{\boldsymbol{x}^{(l-1)}},{\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(l-1)}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\frac{\sigma_{w}^{2}}{d_{l}}\left\langle\sqrt{d_{l}}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{z}^{(l)}}\boldsymbol{z}^{(L)},\sqrt{d_{l}}\nabla_{\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(l)}}\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(L)}\right\rangle\frac{1}{{\tilde{d}_{l-1}}}\left\langle{\boldsymbol{x}^{(l-1)}},{\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(l-1)}}\right\rangle\penalty\ .

The occurring backpropagation terms can be recursively unrolled as

\displaystyle\nabla_{\boldsymbol{z}^{(L)}}\boldsymbol{z}^{(l)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{w}}{\sqrt{d_{l}}}\boldsymbol{W}^{(l+1)\top}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{z}^{(l+1)}}\boldsymbol{z}^{(L)}\odot\varphi^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{z}^{(l)})

\displaystyle 1\leq l\leq L-1\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle\nabla_{\boldsymbol{z}^{(L)}}\boldsymbol{z}^{(L)}

\displaystyle=1,

where
\odot
denotes component-wise multiplication.

In order to rigorously calculate the NNGP and the NTK, we use the simplified
NETSOR
⊤
program from Section 7 in
Yang 2020
.
To this end, define the set of
d
-dimensional initial vectors
\mathcal{V}\coloneqq\{\sigma_{w}\boldsymbol{W}^{(1)}\boldsymbol{x},\sigma_{w}\boldsymbol{W}^{(1)}\bar{\boldsymbol{x}},\boldsymbol{W}^{(L)\top},\sigma_{i}\sigma_{b}\boldsymbol{b}^{(1)},\sigma_{i}\sigma_{b}\boldsymbol{b}^{(2)},\dots,\sigma_{i}\sigma_{b}\boldsymbol{b}^{(L-1)}\}
.
For
1\leq i\leq d
,
the process

(v_{i})_{v\in\mathcal{V}}

is a centered Gaussian process distributed as
(Z^{v})_{v\in\mathcal{V}}
, where
2
2

2

Note that
Z^{v}
is a real-valued random variable for
v\in\mathcal{V}
.

\Cov(Z^{\boldsymbol{W}^{(1)}\boldsymbol{y}},Z^{\boldsymbol{W}^{(1)}\bar{\boldsymbol{y}}})=\left\langle\boldsymbol{y},\bar{\boldsymbol{y}}\right\rangle
for
\boldsymbol{y},\bar{\boldsymbol{y}}\in\{\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}\}
,
\Cov(Z^{\boldsymbol{b}^{(l)}},Z^{v})=\mathds{1}_{\{\boldsymbol{b}^{(l)}\}}(v)
and
\Cov\left(Z^{\boldsymbol{W}^{(L)\top}},v\right)=\mathds{1}_{\{\boldsymbol{W}^{(L)\top}\}}(v)
.
Define the set of
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
random matrices
\mathcal{W}\coloneqq\{{\tilde{\boldsymbol{W}}}^{(2)},\dots,{\tilde{\boldsymbol{W}}}^{(L-1)}\}
by
{\tilde{\boldsymbol{W}}}^{(i)}_{jk}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{w}^{2}/d)
.
Now we recursively define the vectors

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{h}^{(1)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sigma_{w}\boldsymbol{W}^{(1)}\boldsymbol{x},

\displaystyle\,\boldsymbol{h}^{(l)}\coloneqq\tilde{\boldsymbol{W}}^{(l)}\boldsymbol{x}^{(l-1)}

(D.3)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}^{(l)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\boldsymbol{h}^{(l)}+\sigma_{b}\sigma_{i}\boldsymbol{b}^{(l)},

\displaystyle\,\boldsymbol{x}^{(l)}\coloneqq\varphi(\boldsymbol{z}^{(l)}),

\displaystyle\,\operatorname{d}\!\boldsymbol{z}^{(L-1)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\boldsymbol{W}^{(L)\top}\odot\varphi^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{z}^{(L-1)}),\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad

\displaystyle\,\operatorname{d}\!\boldsymbol{h}^{(l)}\coloneqq{\tilde{\boldsymbol{W}}}^{(l+1)\top}\,\operatorname{d}\!\boldsymbol{z}^{(l+1)},

\displaystyle\,\operatorname{d}\!\boldsymbol{z}^{(l)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\,\operatorname{d}\!\boldsymbol{h}^{(l)}\odot\varphi^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{z}^{(l)}),

where the index
l
has the range
1\leq l\leq L-1
for
\boldsymbol{x}^{(l)}
,
1\leq l\leq L-2
for
\,\operatorname{d}\!\boldsymbol{h}^{(l)},\,\operatorname{d}\!\boldsymbol{z}^{(l)}
and
2\leq l\leq L-1
for
\boldsymbol{h}^{(l)},\boldsymbol{z}^{(l)}
.
Also note
\,\operatorname{d}\!\boldsymbol{z}^{(l)}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{z}^{(l)}}\boldsymbol{z}^{(L)}
for
1\leq l\leq L-1
due to the „missing normalization“in the definition of
\,\operatorname{d}\!\boldsymbol{z}^{(L-1)}
.
The individual vectors in
Eq.

D.3
are given by a non-linear operation or a matrix operation as in
(
Yang 2020
, Box 1 p. 7)
.
This enables applying Theorem 7.2 and Box 1 from
Yang 2020
, which states that limits of the kernels

\displaystyle B^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{1}{d}\left\langle\boldsymbol{z}^{(l)},\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(l)}\right\rangle,

\displaystyle C^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{1}{d}\left\langle\boldsymbol{x}^{(l)},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(l)}\right\rangle,

\displaystyle D^{(l,L)}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{1}{d}\left\langle\,\operatorname{d}\!\boldsymbol{z}^{(l)},\,\operatorname{d}\!\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(l)}\right\rangle,

\displaystyle E^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{1}{d}\left\langle\varphi^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{z}^{(l)}),\varphi^{\prime}(\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(l)})\right\rangle.

exist almost surely and furthermore yields recursive formulas for those limits.
Here, we emphasize the network’s depth
L
as an extra upper index for the backpropagation terms
D^{(l,L)}
.
Note that by the independence of the parameters in the network we have

\displaystyle k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})=\lim_{d\to\infty}\Cov\big(\boldsymbol{z}^{(L)},\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}^{(L)}\big)

\displaystyle=\,\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{\sigma_{w}^{2}}{d}\mathbb{E}\big[\boldsymbol{x}^{(L-1)\top}\boldsymbol{W}^{(L)^{\top}}\boldsymbol{W}^{(L)}\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(L-1)}\big]+\sigma_{i}^{2}\sigma_{b}^{2}\mathbb{E}\big[(\boldsymbol{b}^{(L)})^{2}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\lim_{d\to\infty}\sigma_{w}^{2}\mathbb{E}\frac{1}{d}\left\langle\boldsymbol{x}^{(L-1)},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(L-1)}\right\rangle+\sigma_{i}^{2}\sigma_{b}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\sigma_{w}^{2}C^{(L-1)}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})+\sigma_{i}^{2}\sigma_{b}^{2}\penalty\ .

Here, the convergence of the expectation follows from
Yang 2019a
.
By
Eqs.

D.1
and
D.2
the NTK-kernel can be expressed as

\displaystyle k^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{L}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})

\displaystyle=\sigma_{w}^{2}\sum_{l=1}^{L}D^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})C^{(l-1)}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})+\sigma_{b}^{2}\sum_{l=1}^{L}D^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})\penalty\ .

For a kernel
k
denote
\Sigma_{k}(\boldsymbol{y},\bar{\boldsymbol{y}})\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}k(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{y})&k(\boldsymbol{y},\bar{\boldsymbol{y}})\\
k(\boldsymbol{y},\bar{\boldsymbol{y}})&k(\bar{\boldsymbol{y}},\bar{\boldsymbol{y}})\end{pmatrix}
.
Using
Eq.

D.3
and
Yang 2020
, the above kernels are recursively given by

\displaystyle B^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})

\displaystyle=\sigma_{w}^{2}C^{l-1}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})+\sigma_{b}^{2}\sigma_{i}^{2},

(D.4)

\displaystyle C^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{(u,v)\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{B^{(l)}}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}))}\left[\varphi(u)\varphi(v)\right],\quad

\displaystyle(l\geq 1)

\displaystyle C^{(0)}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})

\displaystyle=\left\langle\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}\right\rangle,

\displaystyle D^{(l,L)}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})

\displaystyle=\sigma_{w}^{2}D^{{(l+1)}}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})E^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}),\quad

\displaystyle l\leq L-1,

\displaystyle D^{(L,L)}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle E^{(l)}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{(u,v)\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{B^{(l)}}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}))}[\varphi^{\prime}(u)\varphi^{\prime}(v)],

and a recursive formula for
D^{(l,L)}
over the depth
L
follows as

D^{{(l,L+1)}}=\sigma_{w}^{2}E^{(l)}\cdot\ldots\cdot\sigma_{w}^{2}E^{(L)}=\sigma_{w}^{2}E^{(L)}D^{(l,L)}\penalty\ .

The recursive formula for
k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})
can directly be derived from
Eq.

D.4
. We investigate
k^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{L}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})
by induction.
For
L=1
there is nothing to show.
Assume that the claimed formula holds for
L\in\mathbb{N}
. Then we have

\displaystyle k^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{L+1}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})

\displaystyle=\sigma_{w}^{2}\sum_{l=1}^{L+1}D^{(l,L+1)}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})C^{(l-1)}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})x+\sigma_{b}^{2}\sum_{l=1}^{L}D^{(l,L+1)}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})

\displaystyle=\sigma_{w}^{2}E^{(L)}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})\left(\sigma_{w}^{2}\sum_{l=1}^{L}D^{(l,L)}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})C^{(l-1)}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})+\sigma_{b}^{2}\sum_{l=1}^{L}D^{(l,L)}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})\right)

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ +\sigma_{w}^{2}C^{(L)}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})+\sigma_{b}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{b}^{2}(1-\sigma_{i}^{2})+k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L+1}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})+\sigma_{w}^{2}\mathbb{E}_{(u,v)\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{L})}\left[\varphi^{\prime}(u)\varphi^{\prime}(v)\right]k^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{L}(x,x^{\prime}).\quad\qed

A dot-product kernel
k
on the sphere
\mathbb{S}^{d}
is conveniently described by a function
\kappa:[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}
by defining
\kappa(\left\langle x,y\right\rangle)\coloneqq k(x,y)
. We translate the NNGP- and NTK-recursion of
Lemma

D.2
to a recursion for the corresponding functions
\kappa
describing the restriction of these kernels to the sphere.
See
A.4

Proof.

As
\sigma_{w}^{2}>0
and
\varphi
is not almost surely equal to zero,
\alpha_{l}>0
follows for all
l
. By a simple induction we see that
\alpha_{l}=k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{l}(1)
holds for all
l\geq 1
. The identities for the NNGP can be shown by induction on
l
as well. The induction base
k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{1}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})=\kappa^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{1}(\left\langle\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}\right\rangle)
is straightforward. For the induction step

Lemma

D.2
yields

\displaystyle k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{l}(\boldsymbol{x},{\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}})

\displaystyle=\sigma_{b}^{2}\sigma_{i}^{2}+\sigma_{w}^{2}\mathbb{E}_{(u,v)\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{l-1}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}))}\left[\varphi(u)\varphi(v)\right],

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{l-1}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{l-1}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x})&k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{l-1}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})\\
k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{l-1}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})&k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{l-1}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})\end{pmatrix}={\alpha_{l-1}}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}1&\kappa^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{l-1}(\left\langle\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}\right\rangle)/\alpha_{l-1}\\
\kappa^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{l-1}(\left\langle\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}\right\rangle)/\alpha_{l-1}&1\end{pmatrix}

and the identity
k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{l}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})=\kappa^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{l}(\left\langle\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}\right\rangle)
follows directly from the definition of the dual activation (
A.3
).
A similar argument shows
k^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{l}(\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}})=\kappa^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{l}(\left\langle\boldsymbol{x},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}\right\rangle)
.
∎

D.2
Even and odd parts of neural kernels

Lemma D.3

(Even/odd algebra)
.

Let
f,g:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
. Then,

1.

\left(f+g\right)_{\mathrm{even}}=f_{\mathrm{even}}+g_{\mathrm{even}}
and
\left(f+g\right)_{\mathrm{odd}}=f_{\mathrm{odd}}+g_{\mathrm{odd}}
.

2.

\left(f\cdot g\right)_{\mathrm{even}}=f_{\mathrm{even}}\cdot g_{\mathrm{even}}+f_{\mathrm{odd}}\cdot g_{\mathrm{odd}}
and
\left(f\cdot g\right)_{\mathrm{odd}}=f_{\mathrm{even}}\cdot g_{\mathrm{odd}}+f_{\mathrm{odd}}\cdot g_{\mathrm{even}}
.

3.

If
g
is odd,
\left(f\circ g\right)_{\mathrm{even}}=f_{\mathrm{even}}\circ g
and
\left(f\circ g\right)_{\mathrm{odd}}=f_{\mathrm{odd}}\circ g
. If
g
is even,
f\circ g
is even.

4.

If
f^{\prime}
is a weak derivative of
f
, then
\left(f^{\prime}\right)_{\mathrm{even}}
is a weak derivative of
f_{\mathrm{odd}}
and
\left(f^{\prime}\right)_{\mathrm{odd}}
is a weak derivative of
f_{\mathrm{even}}
.

Proof.

Statements (a) – (c) are straightforward to prove. For (d), we obtain for
x\in\mathbb{R}
:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{x}\left(f^{\prime}\right)_{\mathrm{even}}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{x}\frac{f^{\prime}(t)+f^{\prime}(-t)}{2}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(\int_{0}^{x}f^{\prime}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{x}f^{\prime}(-t)\,\mathrm{d}t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(\int_{0}^{x}f^{\prime}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t-\int_{0}^{-x}f^{\prime}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}((f(x)-f(0))-(f(-x)-f(0)))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{odd}}(x)\penalty\ .

This shows that
\left(f^{\prime}\right)_{\mathrm{even}}
is a weak derivative of
f_{\mathrm{odd}}
, and a similar computation shows that
\left(f^{\prime}\right)_{\mathrm{odd}}
is a weak derivative of
f_{\mathrm{even}}
.
∎

Proposition D.4

(Special cases for NNGP/NTK with even/odd functions)
.

Let the activation function
\varphi
fulfill
5
.

1.

Let
\sigma_{b}^{2}\sigma_{i}^{2}=0
. If
\varphi
is even/odd, then
\kappa^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{l}
is even/odd for all
l\geq 2
.

2.

Let
\sigma_{b}^{2}\sigma_{i}^{2}=0
.
Then,
\left(\kappa^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{2,\varphi}\right)_{\mathrm{even}}=\kappa^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{2,\varphi_{\mathrm{even}}}
and
\left(\kappa^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{2,\varphi}\right)_{\mathrm{odd}}=\kappa^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{2,\varphi_{\mathrm{odd}}}
.

(c)

Let
\sigma_{b}^{2}=0
. If
\varphi
is even/odd, then
\kappa^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{l}
is even/odd for all
l\geq 2
.

(d)

Let
\sigma_{b}^{2}=0
. Then,
\left(\kappa^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{2,\varphi}\right)_{\mathrm{even}}=\kappa^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{2,\varphi_{\mathrm{even}}}
and
\left(\kappa^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{2,\varphi}\right)_{\mathrm{odd}}=\kappa^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{2,\varphi_{\mathrm{odd}}}
.

Here we denote the activation function in the index to clarify the network architecture the kernels belong to.

Proof.

is odd. If

also

\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt

D.3
Adapting the main theorem from
Bietti and Bach 2021

Here, we derive
Theorem

A.2
, an adapted version of the main theorem of
Bietti and Bach 2021
that is closer to our notation. First, we restate the original theorem with minor adaptations (using
\tau
, expanding at
t=0
instead of
t\in\{-1,1\}
, rewriting derivatives of powers, using
\mathbb{S}^{d}
instead of
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
). Here, the notation
a_{n}\sim b_{n}
means
\lim_{n\to\infty}a_{n}/b_{n}=1
.

Theorem D.5

(Theorem 7 in
Bietti and Bach 2021
, arXiv version 4)
.

Let
k:[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}
be a function that is
C^{\infty}
on
(-1,1)
and has for
\tau\in\{1,-1\}
the following expansion for
t\searrow 0

:

\displaystyle k(\tau(1-t))=p_{\tau}(t)+\sum_{j=1}^{r}c_{j,\tau}t^{\nu_{j}}+O(t^{\nu_{1}+1+\varepsilon}),

(D.5)

where
p_{\tau}
are polynomials and
0<\nu_{1}<\dots<\nu_{r}
are not integers and
0<\varepsilon<\nu_{2}-\nu_{1}
. Assume further that the derivatives
k^{(s)}
have for any
s\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
the following expressions for
t\searrow 0
, where
\left(t^{\nu_{j}}\right)^{(s)}
is the
s
-th derivative of
t\mapsto t^{\nu_{j}}
:

\displaystyle k^{(s)}(\tau(1-t))=p_{s,\tau}(t)+\sum_{j=1}^{r}c_{j,\tau}\left(t^{\nu_{j}}\right)^{(s)}+O\left(t^{\nu_{1}+1+\varepsilon-s}\right)

(D.6)

for some polynomials
p_{s,\tau}
. Then, for
d\geq 1
, the eigenvalues
\mu_{k}=\mu_{k}(\kappa,d)
as defined in
Section

2
satisfy, for an absolute constant
C(d,\nu_{1})
,

•

For
k
even, if
c_{1,1}\neq-c_{1,-1}
:
\mu_{k}\sim(c_{1,1}+c_{1,-1})C(d,\nu_{1})k^{-d-2\nu_{1}}
;

•

For
k
even, if
c_{1,1}=-c_{1,-1}
:
\mu_{k}=o(k^{-d-2\nu_{1}})
;

•

For
k
odd, if
c_{1,1}\neq c_{1,-1}
:
\mu_{k}\sim(c_{1,1}-c_{1,-1})C(d,\nu_{1})k^{-\tilde{d}-2\nu_{1}}
;

•

For
k
odd, if
c_{1,1}=c_{1,-1}
:
\mu_{k}=o(k^{-d-2\nu_{1}})
.

We reformulate this in terms of
\mathcal{Q}
, see
A.1
. This perspective allows conveniently handling the
O(t^{\nu_{1}+1+\varepsilon})
-term and its derivatives.
For greater precision, we use the following variant of Lemma B.2 in
Haas et al. 2023
to investigate the sign of eigenvalues
\mu_{i}
:

Lemma D.6

(Guaranteeing strictly positive eigenvalues for kernels on spheres)
.

Let
\kappa:[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}
be continuous, let
d\geq 1
and consider the radial kernels

\displaystyle k_{d}

\displaystyle:\mathbb{S}^{d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d}\to\mathbb{R},k_{d}(x,y)\coloneqq\kappa(\left\langle x,y\right\rangle)

\displaystyle k_{d+2}

\displaystyle:\mathbb{S}^{d+2}\times\mathbb{S}^{d+2}\to\mathbb{R},k_{d+2}(x,y)\coloneqq\kappa(\left\langle x,y\right\rangle).

Suppose that
k_{d+2}
is a kernel. Then,
k_{d}
is a kernel.
If an eigenvalue
\mu_{\hat{l}}
of
k_{d}
fulfills
\mu_{\hat{l}}>0
and
\hat{l}
is even/odd, then for all even/odd
l\leq\hat{l}

\displaystyle\mu_{l}>0

(D.7)

follows.
Especially, if
Eq.

D.7
holds for infinitely many even/odd
l
, then it holds for all even/odd
l
.

The only difference between Lemma B.2 in
Haas et al. 2023
and the above lemma is that the eigenvalues are described in greater detail here, the proof remains the same.

See
A.2

Proof.

Set
\gamma\coloneqq\beta+2
. Fix
\tau\in\{-1,1\}
. Then, we can write
\kappa(\tau(1-t))=b_{\tau}t^{\beta}+p(t)+r(t)
for some
p\in\mathcal{P}_{-1,\beta}
and
r\in\mathcal{R}_{\gamma}(t)
. To apply
Theorem

D.5
, we can rewrite
b_{\tau}t^{\beta}=p_{\tau}(t)+\sum_{j=1}^{r}c_{j,\tau}t^{\nu_{j}}
with
\nu_{1}=\beta
and
c_{1,\tau}=b_{\tau}
. The rest term
r(t)
is covered by the
O(t^{\nu_{1}+1+\varepsilon})
term in
Theorem

D.5
by setting
\varepsilon\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\min\{1,\nu_{2}-\nu_{1}\}
, and the expressions for the derivatives in
Theorem

D.5
are satisfied by the definition of
\mathcal{R}_{\gamma}
.

Hence, we can apply
Theorem

D.5
to obtain the correct asymptotic decay rates, and it remains to show that in the cases (a) and (c), all eigenvalues
\mu_{l}
are strictly positive for all
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. This claim follows from the following considerations:

•

C(d,\nu_{1})\neq 0
: While this is not shown in
Bietti and Bach 2021
, it follows by considering
\tilde{\kappa}(t)\coloneqq(1-t)^{\nu_{1}}
:
This choice of
\kappa
satisfies the assumptions of
Theorem

D.5
with
c_{1,1}=1\neq 0=c_{1,-1}
, hence the cases (a) or respectively (c) apply.

Suppose
C(d,\nu_{1})=0
, then we obtain
\mu_{k}\sim 0
and hence only finitely many
\mu_{k}
are nonzero. Following Appendix A in
Bietti and Bach 2021
,
\tilde{\kappa}
can be expressed as

\displaystyle\tilde{\kappa}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\mu_{l}N_{d,l}P_{l}(t)\penalty\ ,

where the
P_{l}
are Legendre polynomials of degree
l
for the dimension
d+1
.
Hence,
\tilde{\kappa}
would be a polynomial, contradicting the definition
\tilde{\kappa}(t)=(1-t)^{\nu_{1}}
.

•

Since we assumed
k_{\kappa,d}
to be a (positive semi-definite) kernel, we cannot have negative eigenvalues, hence
(c_{1,1}+c_{1,-1})C(d,\nu_{1})>0
or respectively
(c_{1,1}-c_{1,-1})C(d,\nu_{1})>0
follows in the cases (a) and (c).

•

The notation
\sim
from
Theorem

D.5
allows a finite number of
\mu_{l}
to be zero.
However, as there are infinitely many both even and odd indices for which
\mu_{l}
is strictly positive,
Lemma

D.6
shows that
\mu_{l}>0
holds for all
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

∎

D.4
Eigenvalue decay

Some preliminary constructions useful for both the NNGP and the NTK kernel are first established here, stressing the perspective of layerwise computation in the neural network.

We will study
\kappa^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L},\kappa^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{L}:[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}
as compositions of functions which break the kernels given by depth
L
down to a
“composition”
of kernels of depth
1
.

Recall the recursive form of the NNGP-kernel on the sphere given in
Lemma

A.4
and the dual activation function

\varphi

\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt

in
A.3
.

Notation D.7

(NNGP- and NTK related terms)
.

Recursively define the functions
g_{l},G_{l},H_{l}:[-1,1]\to[-1,1]
by

\displaystyle\alpha_{l}

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle\kappa^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{l,\varphi}(1),

\displaystyle g_{1}(t)

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma_{b}^{2}\sigma_{i}^{2}+\sigma_{w}^{2}t}{\alpha_{1}},

\displaystyle g_{l}(t)

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma_{b}^{2}\sigma_{i}^{2}+\sigma_{w}^{2}\hbox{\hskip 411.22394pt\hskip-11.64052pt\hbox{$\varphi_{\cdot\sqrt{\alpha_{l-1}}}$}\hskip-11.64052pt\hskip-411.22394pt\raisebox{-0.65001pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.11778pt}{\resizebox{48365776}{677201}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94691pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-411.22394pt\hskip 411.22394pt}(t)}{\alpha_{l}},

\displaystyle G_{l}(t)

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle g_{l}\circ\dots\circ g_{1}(t),

\displaystyle H_{1}(t)

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle\sigma_{b}^{2}+\sigma_{w}^{2}t,

\displaystyle H_{l}(t)

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle\sigma_{b}^{2}(1-\sigma_{i}^{2})+\alpha_{l}G_{l}(t)+\sigma_{w}^{2}\hbox{\hskip 411.22394pt\hskip-11.64052pt\hbox{$\varphi^{\prime}_{\cdot\sqrt{\alpha_{l-1}}}$}\hskip-11.64052pt\hskip-411.22394pt\raisebox{2.56227pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.11778pt}{\resizebox{48365776}{677201}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94691pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 411.22394pt}\left(G_{l-1}(t)\right)H_{l-1}(t)\penalty\ .

∎

By the recursive formulation of
\kappa^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{l},\kappa^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{l}
in
Lemma

A.4
we observe
for all
t\in[-1,1]

\displaystyle\alpha_{l}G_{l}(t)

\displaystyle=\kappa^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{l}(t),

\displaystyle H_{l}(t)

\displaystyle=\kappa^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{l}(t)\penalty\ .

Lemma D.8
.

Under the assumptions of
Lemma

A.4
, we have for any
l\geq 1

\displaystyle\max_{t\in[-1,1]}\left\lvert g_{l}(t)\right\rvert=g_{l}(1)=1\penalty\ .

Furthermore,

\displaystyle G_{l}[-1,1)\subset(-1,1)

holds for all
l\geq 1
when
\sigma_{b}^{2}\sigma_{i}^{2}>0
, and for all
l\geq 2
when
\varphi
is neither even nor odd.

Proof.

Follows from the definition of
g_{l}
and
Lemma

C.3
.
∎

Lemma D.9

(Behavior at
1
)
.

Let the activation function
\varphi
fulfill
5
and
let
m\coloneqq\inf\{k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\mid\varphi^{(k)}(0+)\neq\varphi^{(k)}(0-)\}
be its smoothness, see
7
.
Let
l\geq 2
.

1.

Let
m=0
.
Then we have for
t\searrow 0

\displaystyle g_{l}(1-t)

\displaystyle=1-\left(c_{l}t^{1/2}+\mathcal{Q}_{0,1/2}(t)\right),

\displaystyle G_{l}(1-t)

\displaystyle=1-\left(C_{l}t^{2^{1-l}}+\mathcal{Q}_{0,2^{1-l}}(t)\right),

for constants
c_{l},C_{l}>0
.

2.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
. Then we have for
t\searrow 0

\displaystyle g_{l}(1-t)

\displaystyle=1-\left(b_{l}t+(-1)^{m}c_{l}t^{m+1/2}+\mathcal{Q}_{1,{m+1/2}}(t)\right),

\displaystyle G_{l}(1-t)

\displaystyle=1-\left(B_{l}t+(-1)^{m}C_{l}t^{m+1/2}+\mathcal{Q}_{1,{m+1/2}}(t)\right),

\displaystyle H_{l}(1-t)

\displaystyle=A_{l}-\left((-1)^{m+1}D_{l}t^{m-1/2}+\mathcal{Q}_{0,m-1/2}(t)\right)

for constants
b_{l}=g_{l}^{\prime}(1),c_{l},A_{l},B_{l},C_{l},D_{l},>0
.
Furthermore, we have for
l\geq 3
the recursive relation
C_{l}\geq g^{\prime}_{l}(1)C_{l-1}
.

3.

Let
m=\infty
. Then we have for any
\tau\in\{\pm 1\},q\in\mathbb{R}
and
t\searrow 0

\displaystyle g_{l}(\tau(1-t))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}_{-1,q}(t),

\displaystyle G_{l}(\tau(1-t))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}_{-1,q}(t),

\displaystyle H_{l}(\tau(1-t))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}_{-1,q}(t).

Proof.

1.

The formula for
g_{l}
follows from
Theorem

A.6
and
Lemma

D.8
. We obtain the formula for
G_{l}
via induction, where in the base case for
l=1
by definition
G_{1}(1-t)=1-c_{1}t
with
c_{1}>0
holds.
For
l=2
we have

\displaystyle G_{2}(1-t)

\displaystyle=g_{2}(G_{1}(1-t))=g_{2}(1-(1-G_{1}(1-t)))=1-c_{2}c_{1}^{1/2}t^{1/2}+\mathcal{Q}_{0,1/2}\left(c_{1}t\right)

as claimed. For
l\geq 3
,
note that the identities
\mathcal{Q}_{0,2^{2-l}}(t)=\mathcal{Q}_{2^{2-l},2^{2-l}}(t)
and
\mathcal{Q}_{0,1/2}(t)=\mathcal{Q}_{1/2,1/2}(t)
hold by
A.1
. From
Proposition

B.2
(e,f) we obtain

\displaystyle G_{l}(1-t)

\displaystyle=g_{l}(G_{l-1}(1-t))=g_{l}(1-(1-G_{l-1}(1-t)))

\displaystyle=1-c_{l}\left(C_{l-1}t^{2^{2-l}}+\mathcal{Q}_{2^{2-l},2^{2-l}}(t)\right)^{1/2}+\mathcal{Q}_{1/2,1/2}\left(C_{l-1}t^{2^{2-l}}+\mathcal{Q}_{2^{2-l},2^{2-l}}(t)\right)

\displaystyle=1-\left(C_{l}t^{2^{1-l}}+\mathcal{Q}_{2^{1-l},2^{1-l}}(t)\right)

and the claim follows from the identity
\mathcal{Q}_{2^{1-l},2^{1-l}}(t)=\mathcal{Q}_{0,2^{1-l}}(t)
.

2.

Define
\beta\coloneqq m+1/2
.
From
Theorem

A.6
we obtain

\displaystyle g_{l}(1-t)=(-1)^{m+1}c_{l}t^{\beta}+\mathcal{Q}_{-1,\beta}(t)

with
c_{l}>0
.
Lemma

D.8
shows
g_{l}(0)=1
and furthermore we observe

\displaystyle\hbox{\hskip 411.22394pt\hskip-11.64052pt\hbox{$\varphi_{\cdot\sqrt{\alpha_{l-1}}}$}\hskip-11.64052pt\hskip-411.22394pt\raisebox{-0.65001pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.11778pt}{\resizebox{48365776}{677201}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94691pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94725pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.97221pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-411.22394pt\hskip 411.22394pt}^{\prime}(1)=\mathbb{E}_{u\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\alpha_{l-1})}[\varphi^{\prime}(u)^{2}]>0

as
\varphi
is not constant, since
1\leq m<\infty
. From
Lemma

D.8
and using
b_{l}\coloneqq g_{l}^{\prime}(1)>0
, it follows that

\displaystyle g_{l}(1-t)

\displaystyle=1-\left(b_{l}t+(-1)^{m}c_{l}t^{m+1/2}+\mathcal{Q}_{1,{m+1/2}}(t)\right)\penalty\ .

Note that for the NNGP term
G_{l}
we have to argue more carefully to obtain the strictly positive factor
B_{l}>0
for the linear term, while this is not required for the NTK term
H_{l}
where we can rely on the more precisely calculated NNGP term.
We show the formula for
G_{l}
via induction. For
l=2
it follows directly from
G_{2}=g_{2}\circ g_{1}
using
g_{1}(1-t)=1-c_{1}t
.
In the induction step, let
l\geq 3
and define

\displaystyle\bar{G}_{l-1}(t)\coloneqq 1-G_{l-1}(t)=B_{l-1}t+(-1)^{m}C_{l-1}t^{\beta}+\mathcal{Q}_{1,\beta}(t)\penalty\ .

We rewrite

\displaystyle G_{l}(1-t)

\displaystyle=g_{l}(G_{l-1}(1-t))=g_{l}(1-\bar{G}_{l-1}(t))

\displaystyle=1-\left(b_{l}\bar{G}_{l-1}(t)+(-1)^{m}c_{l}\left(\bar{G}_{l-1}(t)\right)^{\beta}+\mathcal{Q}_{1,\beta}\left(\bar{G}_{l-1}(t)\right)\right)

and investigate the single summands. The linear summand straightforwardly yields

\displaystyle b_{l}\bar{G}_{l-1}(t)=b_{l}B_{l-1}t+(-1)^{m}b_{l}C_{l-1}t^{\beta}+\mathcal{Q}_{1,\beta}(t).

The power summand can be handled with
Proposition

B.2
(e), where we write
\bar{G}_{l-1}=b_{l}B_{l-1}t+\mathcal{Q}_{1,1}(t)
and consequently we have

\displaystyle(\bar{G}_{l-1}(t))^{\beta}

\displaystyle=(b_{l}B_{l-1})^{\beta}t^{\beta}+\mathcal{Q}_{\beta,\beta}(t)

\displaystyle=(b_{l}B_{l-1})^{\beta}t^{\beta}+\mathcal{Q}_{1,\beta}(t)\penalty\ .

In order to calculate
\mathcal{Q}_{1,\beta}(\bar{G}_{l-1}(t))
we decompose
\mathcal{Q}_{1,\beta}(t)=p(t)+q(t)
, where
p(t)
is a polynomial with integer powers greater than
1
and
q(t)\in\mathcal{Q}_{\beta,\beta}(t)
.
Using
Proposition

B.2
(d) we have, as
p(0)=p^{\prime}(0)=0
holds,

\displaystyle p(\bar{G}_{L-1}(t))=p(B_{l-1}t+(-1)^{m}C_{l-1}t^{\beta}+\mathcal{Q}_{1,\beta}(t))=\mathcal{Q}_{1,\beta}(t)\penalty\ .

Proposition

B.2
(f) yields
q(t)=\mathcal{Q}_{\beta,\beta}(t)=\mathcal{Q}_{1,\beta}(t)
and we obtain

\mathcal{Q}_{1,\beta}\left(\bar{G}_{l-1}(t)\right)=\mathcal{Q}_{1,\beta}(t)
.
Altogether, we see that

\displaystyle G_{l}(1-t)=1-\big(b_{l}B_{l-1}t+(-1)^{m}(b_{l}C_{l-1}(b_{l}B_{l-1})^{\beta})t^{\beta}+\mathcal{Q}_{1,\beta}(t)\big)

holds as desired, and furthermore we have

\displaystyle C_{l}\coloneqq b_{l}C_{l-1}(b_{l}B_{l-1})^{\beta}\geq b_{l}C_{l-1}=g_{l}^{\prime}(1)C_{l-1}\penalty\ .

We move on to calculate
H_{l}
for
l\geq 2
.
An elementary argument yields
\kappa^{\mathrm{NTK}}(1)>0
as a consequence of
\sigma_{w}>0
, hence we have
A_{l}>0
for all
l\geq 2
.
Again, the induction is based on the recursive construction in
D.7
, where we handle the term arising from

\varphi^{\prime}_{\cdot\sqrt{\alpha_{l}}}

\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt

using
Theorem

A.6
.
Since
\varphi^{\prime}
has smoothness
m-1
we obtain

\displaystyle g_{l}^{\prime}(1-t)=\hbox{\hskip 411.22394pt\hskip-8.75853pt\hbox{$\varphi^{\prime}_{\cdot\sqrt{\alpha_{l}}}$}\hskip-8.75853pt\hskip-411.22394pt\raisebox{2.56227pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.11778pt}{\resizebox{48365776}{677201}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94691pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 411.22394pt}(1-t)=a_{l}(-1)^{m}t^{\beta-1}+\mathcal{Q}_{-1,\beta-1}(t)

for some

.
For
l=2
, the claimed form of
H_{l}
now follows straightforwardly.
For the induction step, assume
l\geq 3
.
Then we have

\displaystyle\hbox{\hskip Theorem D.10
.

Let the assumptions of
Lemma

A.4
be satisfied. Choose a number of layers
L\geq 2
and a parity
r\in\{0,1\}
of the eigenvalues to be analyzed. Define
\varphi^{[0]}\coloneqq\varphi_{\mathrm{even}}
and
\varphi^{[1]}\coloneqq\varphi_{\mathrm{odd}}
.

(NNGP)

Define the simplified activation
\tilde{\varphi}
by

\displaystyle\tilde{\varphi}

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\varphi^{[r]}&,\text{ if $\sigma_{b}^{2}\sigma_{i}^{2}=0$ and ($L=2$ or $\varphi$ is even or odd)}\\
\varphi&,\text{ otherwise,}\end{cases}

and let
s\coloneqq\operatorname{smoothness}(\tilde{\varphi})
.
Note that in all cases, the kernels
k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L}
and
k^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{L}
refer to the
original
activation
\varphi
, while the smoothness parameter
s
is determined by the simplified activation
\tilde{\varphi}
.

(1.1)

If
s=0
, then
\mu_{2t+r,d}(k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L})=\Theta_{\forall t}\left((2t+r+1)^{-d-2^{2-L}}\right)
.

(1.2)

If
1\leq s<\infty
, then
\mu_{2t+r,d}(k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L})=\Theta_{\forall t}\left({(2t+r+1)^{-d-2s-1}}\right)
.

(1.3)

If
s=\infty
and
\tilde{\varphi}
is not a polynomial, then
\mu_{2t+r,d}(k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L})>0
for all
t

and
\mu_{2t+r,d}(k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L})=o_{\forall t}((2t+r+1)^{-q})
for all
q>0
.

(1.4)

If
\tilde{\varphi}
is a polynomial, refer to the polynomial case below.

(NTK)

Define the simplified activation
\tilde{\varphi}
by

\displaystyle\tilde{\varphi}

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\varphi^{[r]}&,\text{ if $\sigma_{b}^{2}=0$ and ($L=2$ or $\varphi$ is even or odd)}\\
\varphi&,\text{ otherwise,}\end{cases}

and let
s\coloneqq\operatorname{smoothness}(\tilde{\varphi})
.

(2.1)

If
1\leq s<\infty
, then
\mu_{2t+r,d}(k^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{L})=\Theta_{\forall t}\left({(2t+r+1)^{-d-2s+1}}\right)
.

(2.2)

If
s=\infty
and
\tilde{\varphi}
is not a polynomial, then
\mu_{2t+r,d}(k^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{L})>0
for all
t
and
\mu_{2t+r,d}(k^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{L})<o_{\forall t}((2t+r+1)^{-q})
for all
q>0
.

(2.3)

If
\tilde{\varphi}
is a polynomial, refer to the polynomial case below.

Polynomial case:

(3.1)

Let the activation
\tilde{\varphi}(t)=\sum_{i\geq 0}\lambda_{i}t^{i}
be a nonzero polynomial.
Define the even/odd degree of
\tilde{\varphi}
as the degree of
\tilde{\varphi}(t)+\tilde{\varphi}(-t)
or of
\tilde{\varphi}(t)-\tilde{\varphi}(-t)
. For the NNGP, define
\sigma\coloneqq\sigma_{b}^{2}\sigma_{i}^{2}
and for the NTK define
\sigma\coloneqq\sigma_{b}^{2}
respectively.

Define
N_{\text{even}},N_{\text{odd}}
according to the following table. By
\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{ev}}\left(\tilde{\varphi}\right),\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{od}}\left(\tilde{\varphi}\right)
we denote the even/odd degree, see
D.11
, of
\tilde{\varphi}
displayed in the Hermite basis, which is possible as
\varphi
fulfills
5
.

N_{\text{even}}

N_{\text{odd}}

\sigma^{2}>0
,
\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{ev}}\left(\tilde{\varphi}\right)>\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{od}}\left(\tilde{\varphi}\right)

\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{ev}}\left(\tilde{\varphi}\right)^{L-1}

\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{ev}}\left({\tilde{\varphi}}\right)^{L-1}-1

\sigma^{2}>0
,
\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{ev}}\left(\tilde{\varphi}\right)<\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{od}}\left({\tilde{\varphi}}\right)

\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{od}}\left({\tilde{\varphi}}\right)^{L-1}-1

\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{od}}\left({\tilde{\varphi}}\right)^{L-1}

\sigma^{2}=0,\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{ev}}\left(\tilde{\varphi}\right)>\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{od}}\left(\tilde{\varphi}\right)

\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{ev}}\left(\tilde{\varphi}\right)^{L-1}

\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{ev}}\left({\tilde{\varphi}}\right)^{L-1}-\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{ev}}\left(\tilde{\varphi}\right)+\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{od}}\left(\tilde{\varphi}\right)

\sigma^{2}=0,\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{ev}}\left(\tilde{\varphi}\right)<\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{od}}\left(\tilde{\varphi}\right)

\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{od}}\left(\tilde{\varphi}\right)^{L-1}-\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{od}}\left(\tilde{\varphi}\right)+\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{ev}}\left(\tilde{\varphi}\right)

\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{od}}\left({\tilde{\varphi}}\right)^{L-1}

Then,

\displaystyle\mu_{2t+r,d}(k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L}/k^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{L})>0\text{ if and only if }\begin{cases}2t+r\leq N_{\text{even}}&,r\text{ even,}\\
2t+r\leq N_{\text{odd}}&,r\text{ odd.}\end{cases}

(3.2)

If
\tilde{\varphi}=0
, then
\mu_{2t+r,d}(k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L})>0
if and only if
2t+r=0
and
\sigma_{b}^{2}\sigma_{i}^{2}>0
, and
\mu_{2t+r,d}(k^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{L})>0
if and only if

\sigma_{b}^{2}>0
.

The definition of
\tilde{\varphi}
may look complicated.
However, in contrast to
\varphi
, it prevents that
c_{1,1}=-(-1)^{r}c_{1,-1}
in
