A.2
, which then would not provide an exact decay rate.

Proof.

For a bounded radial kernel
k(x,y)\coloneqq\kappa(\left\langle x,y\right\rangle)
on
\mathbb{S}^{d}
we define
k_{\text{even}}(x,y)\coloneqq\kappa_{\text{even}}(\left\langle x,y\right\rangle),k_{\text{odd}}(x,y)\coloneqq\kappa_{\text{odd}}(\left\langle x,y\right\rangle)
.
The spherical harmonics of degree
l
are even if
l
is even and odd if
l
is odd.
Hence, we obtain

\displaystyle\mu_{2t,d}(k)

\displaystyle=\mu_{2t,d}(k_{\text{even}}),

\displaystyle\mu_{2t+1,d}(k)

\displaystyle=\mu_{2t+1,d}(k_{\text{odd}})

for all
t\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. For the NNGP- and NTK-kernel,
Proposition

D.4
shows that in the cases
where
\tilde{\varphi}\neq\varphi
holds we have

\displaystyle\kappa^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L,\varphi_{\mathrm{even}}}

\displaystyle=\left(\kappa^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L,\varphi}\right)_{\text{even}},

\displaystyle\kappa^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{L,\varphi_{\mathrm{even}}}

\displaystyle=\left(\kappa^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{L,\varphi}\right)_{\text{even}},

\displaystyle\kappa^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L,\varphi_{\mathrm{odd}}}

\displaystyle=\left(\kappa^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L,\varphi}\right)_{\text{odd}},

\displaystyle\kappa^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{L,\varphi_{\mathrm{odd}}}

\displaystyle=\left(\kappa^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{L,\varphi}\right)_{\text{odd}},

hence we conclude

\displaystyle\mu_{2t+r}\left(k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L,\tilde{\varphi}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mu_{2t+r}\left(k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L,\varphi}\right),

\displaystyle\mu_{2t+r}\left(k^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{L,\tilde{\varphi}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mu_{2t+r}\left(k^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{L,\varphi}\right),

and it suffices to investigate the kernels induced by
\tilde{\varphi}
.
In the following,
g_{l},G_{l}
and
H_{l}
from

refer to the functions induced by the activation function
\tilde{\varphi}
.

(c)

\sigma_{b}^{2}\sigma_{i}^{2}=0

even is not possible since even activation functions

cannot have smoothness

.

(1.3)

For
q>0
choose
\tilde{q}>0,\tilde{q}\not\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
-d-2\tilde{q}\leq-q
.
By
Lemma

D.9
we have for
\tau\in\{1,-1\}
:

\displaystyle G_{L}(\tau(1-t))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Q}_{-1,\tilde{q}}(t)

and
Theorem

A.6
yields

\mu_{2t+r,d}(k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L})=o_{\forall t}((2t+r+1)^{-d-2\tilde{q}})=o_{\forall t}((2t+r+1)^{-q})
.

We proceed to show that all Eigenvalues
\mu_{2t+1,d}(k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L})
are strictly positive.
As
\tilde{\varphi}
is not a polynomial, its Hermite series representation has infinitely many nonzero coefficients; that is, we have
\deg^{\text{Her}}\left(\tilde{\varphi}\right)=\infty
. See
Section

D.5
for more details.
We apply
Lemma

D.13
.

Assume even parity
r=0
. In the case
\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(\tilde{\varphi}\right)=\infty
, the claim follows directly. In the case
\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(\tilde{\varphi}\right)<\deg_{\text{od}}\left(\tilde{\varphi}\right)=\infty
, we note that
\tilde{\varphi}=\varphi
follows and investigate sub-cases.
If
\sigma_{b}^{2}\sigma_{i}^{2}>0
, then
Lemma

D.13
directly yields
\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(G_{l}\right)=\infty
as desired.
If
\sigma_{b}^{2}\sigma_{i}^{2}=0
holds, then the definition of
\tilde{\varphi}
implies that
\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(\tilde{\varphi}\right)\geq 0
and
L\geq 3
hold, and again the claim follows directly from
Lemma

D.13
.
The case of odd parity
r=1
is proven analogously.

(D.10)

(2.1)

Define
\gamma\coloneqq s-1/2
.
Lemma

D.9
yields

\displaystyle H_{L}(1-t)=C_{L,+}t^{\gamma}+\mathcal{Q}_{-1,\gamma}(t)

for

, where

.
By

we need to show

\displaystyle H_{L}(-(1-t))

\displaystyle=C_{L,-}t^{\gamma}+\mathcal{Q}_{-1,\gamma}(t)\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle C_{L,-}

\displaystyle\neq\begin{cases}-C_{L,+}&\text{, if }r=0\\
C_{L+}&\text{, if }r=1\penalty\ .\end{cases}

In case (1.2) we saw for the NNGP-kernel that

holds for

. Hence, it suffices to show

\displaystyle=C_{L,-}t^{\gamma}+\mathcal{Q}_{-1,\gamma}(t)\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle C_{L,-}

(b)

Suppose
\sigma_{b}^{2}=0
,
\tilde{\varphi}
neither even nor odd and
L\geq 3
, that is
\tilde{\varphi}=\varphi
.

Without loss of generality assume
\sigma_{w}^{2}=1
for simpler notation.
We show
\left\lvert C_{2,+}\right\rvert\geq\left\lvert C_{2,-}\right\rvert
, for
l\geq 3
we show that
\left\lvert C_{l-1,+}\right\rvert\geq\left\lvert C_{l-1,-}\right\rvert
implies
\left\lvert C_{l,+}\right\rvert>\left\lvert C_{l,-}\right\rvert
. That yields
Eq.

D.11
. Furthermore we show that
\sign(C_{l,+})=(-1)^{s}
holds, which is required for the corresponding recursion.

As
G_{1}(t)=t
, we have for
\tau=\pm 1
by
Theorem

D.5
A close look at the even/odd degree of
G_{l}
and
H_{l}

In this subsection we develop the methods required to deal with smooth activations in
Theorem

9
, where the only difficulty not covered by
Theorem

A.2
is investigating which eigenvalues of the NNGP-kernel and NTK-kernel are strictly positive.

Definition D.11
.

Let
p(t)=\sum_{n\geq 0}\lambda_{n}t^{n},q(t)=\sum_{n\geq 0}\eta_{n}t^{n}
be
power series
which converge on an interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
.
We define the degree of
p
as
\deg\left(p\right)\coloneqq\sup\left(\{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\mid\lambda_{n}\neq 0\}\cup\{-\infty\}\right)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\cup\{-\infty,\infty\}
and the even/odd degree of
p
as

\displaystyle\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(p\right)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\deg\left(t\mapsto p(t)+p(-t)\right)\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle\deg_{\text{od}}\left(p\right)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\deg\left(t\mapsto p(t)-p(-t)\right)

and say

\displaystyle p=_{\deg}q

if and only if
\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(p\right)=\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(q\right)
and
\deg_{\text{od}}\left(p\right)=\deg_{\text{od}}\left(q\right)
.
That is to say, we compare only the highest even/odd degrees for this notion of equality.

Let
f\in\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{N}(0,1))
be a function. Then
f
equals its convergent
Hermite
representation
t\mapsto\sum_{n\geq 0}a_{n}(f)h_{n}(t)
almost everywhere, and we define
\deg^{\text{Her}}\left(f\right),\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{ev}}\left(f\right),\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{od}}\left(f\right)
and
f=_{\deg}^{\text{Her}}g
based on the Hermite coefficients
(a_{n})_{n\geq 0}

analogously to the above definitions.

Furthermore we also use the notation
f=_{\deg}^{\text{Pow,Her}}g
to denote that the even/odd degree of
f
and
g
coincide when
f
is a power series and and
g
is displayed in the Hermite basis. ∎

Note that
\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(p\right)=-\infty
if
p
is odd and respectively
\deg_{\text{od}}\left(p\right)=-\infty
if
p
is even, resembling the common convention for the degree of polynomials.

Lemma D.12

(Odd/even degree of power series with non-negative coefficients)
.

Let
f,g,\tilde{f},\tilde{g}
be power series with non-negative coefficients converging on an interval
I
, such that the products and compositions below are absolutely convergent on
\bar{I}
.
We use the conventions
\infty-\infty\coloneqq-\infty,0\cdot\infty\coloneqq 0,a\vee b\coloneqq\max\{a,b\}
.

1.

(a)

The sum
g+f
fulfills

\displaystyle\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(g+f\right)

\displaystyle=\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(g\right)\vee\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(f\right),

\displaystyle\deg_{\text{od}}\left(g+f\right)

\displaystyle=\deg_{\text{od}}\left(g\right)\vee\deg_{\text{od}}\left(f\right)\penalty\ .

(b)

The product
g\cdot f
fulfills

\displaystyle\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(g\cdot f\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(g\right)+\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(f\right)\right)\vee\left(\deg_{\text{od}}\left(g\right)+\deg_{\text{od}}\left(f\right)\right),

\displaystyle\deg_{\text{od}}\left(g\cdot f\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(g\right)+\deg_{\text{od}}\left(f\right)\right)\vee\left(\deg_{\text{od}}\left(g\right)+\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(f\right)\right)\penalty\ .

(c)

Let
f\neq 0
. The composition
g\circ f
fulfills

\displaystyle\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(g\circ f\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(g\right)\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(f\right)\right)\vee\left(\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(g\right)\deg_{\text{od}}\left(f\right)\right)

\displaystyle\penalty\ \vee\penalty\ \left(\deg_{\text{od}}\left(g\right)\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(f\right)\right)\vee\left((\deg_{\text{od}}\left(g\right)-1)\deg_{\text{od}}\left(f\right)+\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(f\right)\right)

\displaystyle\deg_{\text{od}}\left(g\circ f\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\deg_{\text{od}}\left(g\right)\deg_{\text{od}}\left(f\right)\right)\vee\left((\deg_{\text{od}}\left(g\right)-1)\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(f\right)+\deg_{\text{od}}\left(f\right)\right)

\displaystyle\penalty\ \vee\penalty\ \left((\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(g\right)-1)\deg_{\text{od}}\left(f\right)+\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(f\right)\right)

\displaystyle\penalty\ \vee\penalty\ \left((\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(g\right)-1)\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(f\right)+\deg_{\text{od}}\left(f\right)\right)\penalty\ .

2.

Let
\tilde{f}=_{\deg}f,\tilde{g}=_{\deg}g
. Then we have

\displaystyle g+f

\displaystyle=_{\deg}\tilde{g}+\tilde{f}\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle g\cdot f

\displaystyle=_{\deg}\tilde{g}\cdot\tilde{f}\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle g\circ f

\displaystyle=_{\deg}\tilde{g}\circ\tilde{f}\penalty\ .

Proof.

1.
The requirement that
f,g
have nonzero coefficients is crucial as it prevents the elimination of coefficients.

We only prove the even-statement in iii) as this is the most difficult statement. The other claims are similar but easier to prove.

As a consequence of the absolute convergence we observe that
g\circ f=\lim_{n\to\infty}g_{n}\circ f_{n}
and
\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(g\circ f\right)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(g_{n}\circ f_{n}\right),\deg_{\text{od}}\left(g\circ f\right)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\deg_{\text{od}}\left(g_{n}\circ f_{n}\right)
holds, where
f_{n}(t)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}\lambda_{i}t^{i},g_{n}(t)=\sum_{j=0}^{n}\mu_{j}t^{j}
.
Hence, we can assume without loss of generality that
\deg f,\deg g<\infty
holds and obtain

\displaystyle g\circ f(t)=\sum_{i\geq 0}\mu_{i}\Big(\sum_{j\geq 0}\lambda_{j}t^{j}\Big)^{i}=\sum_{i\geq 0}\mu_{i}\Big(\sum_{j_{1},\dots,j_{i}\geq 0}\lambda_{j_{1}}\cdot\ldots\cdot\lambda_{j_{i}}t^{j_{1}+\dots+j_{i}}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\sum_{i\geq 0}\sum_{j_{1},\dots,j_{i}\geq 0}\mu_{i}\lambda_{j_{1}}\cdot\ldots\cdot\lambda_{j_{i}}t^{j_{1}+\dots+j_{i}}\penalty\ .

We note that all occurring coefficients are non-negative, as they are products of the non-negative coefficients
\lambda_{j},\mu_{i}
. In the above expressions, there are multiple summands contributing to a power
t^{r}
, however, the coefficient of
t^{r}
in the power series
g\circ f
is nonzero if and only if any of the summands
\mu_{i}\lambda_{j_{1}}\cdot\ldots\cdot\lambda_{j_{i}}
with
j_{1}+\dots+j_{i}=r
is nonzero. Define

\displaystyle I(f)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\mid\lambda_{i}\neq 0\}\cup\{-\infty\},

\displaystyle I(g)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\mid\mu_{i}\neq 0\}\cup\{-\infty\}

and it is immediately clear that the coefficient

\mu_{i}\lambda_{j_{1}}\cdot\ldots\cdot\lambda_{j_{i}}

is nonzero if and only if

i\in I(g)\setminus\{-\infty\},j_{1},\dots,j_{i}\in I(f)\setminus\{-\infty\}\penalty\ .

The reason we add
\{-\infty\}
to
I(f),I(g)
is that it allows dealing with cases such as
f=0
or
f
odd in a simple manner.
In order to investigate
\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(g\circ f\right)
we stepwise simplify:

\displaystyle\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(g\circ f\right)

\displaystyle=\max_{i\in I(g)}

\displaystyle\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1},\dots,j_{i}\in I(f)\\
j_{1}+\dots+j_{i}\text{ even}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle j_{1}+\dots+j_{i}

\displaystyle=\max_{i\in I(g)}

\displaystyle\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{i}\leq i\\
\tilde{i}\text{ even}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle(i-\tilde{i})\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(f\right)+\tilde{i}\deg_{\text{od}}\left(f\right)

(D.22)

\displaystyle=\max\big\{

\displaystyle\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{i}\leq\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(g\right)\\
\tilde{i}\text{ even}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle(\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(g\right)-\tilde{i})\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(f\right)+\tilde{i}\deg_{\text{od}}\left(f\right),

(D.23)

\displaystyle\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{i}\leq\deg_{\text{od}}\left(g\right)\\
\tilde{i}\text{ even}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle(\deg_{\text{od}}\left(g\right)-\tilde{i})\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(f\right)+\tilde{i}\deg_{\text{od}}\left(f\right)\big\}

\displaystyle=\max\big\{

\displaystyle\max\{

\displaystyle\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(g\right)\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(f\right),\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(g\right)\deg_{\text{od}}\left(f\right)\},

(D.24)

\displaystyle\max\{

\displaystyle\deg_{\text{od}}\left(g\right)\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(f\right),\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(f\right)+(\deg_{\text{od}}\left(g\right)-1)\deg_{\text{od}}\left(f\right)\}\big\}\penalty\ .

Here,
Eq.

D.22
is obtained by observing

\displaystyle j_{1}+\dots+j_{i}

\displaystyle\leq\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(f\right)+j_{2}+\dots+j_{i}\quad

\displaystyle\text{, if }j_{1}\text{ is even or respectively}

\displaystyle j_{1}+\dots+j_{i}

\displaystyle\leq\deg_{\text{od}}\left(f\right)+j_{2}+\dots+j_{i}\quad

\displaystyle\text{, if }j_{1}\text{ is odd}\penalty\ ,

where the sum on the right hand side remains even.
Similarly,
Eq.

D.23
follows by observing for
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
the inequality

\displaystyle(i-\tilde{i})\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(f\right)+\tilde{i}\deg_{\text{od}}\left(f\right)

\displaystyle\leq\max\big\{(2m+i-(2m+\tilde{i}))\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(f\right)+(2m+\tilde{i})\deg_{\text{od}}\left(f\right),

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad(2m+i-\tilde{i})\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(f\right)+\tilde{i}\deg_{\text{od}}\left(f\right)\big\}

as by assumption
\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(f\right)\geq 0
or
\deg_{\text{od}}\left(f\right)\geq 1
hold.
Finally,
Eq.

D.24
follows by observing that extremal behavior in
Eq.

D.23
is obtained by the highest or the lowest valid
\tilde{i}
.
The equation for
\deg_{\text{od}}\left(g\circ f\right)
follows analogously.

2.
Follows from 1.
∎

In the following lemma, we investigate the even/odd degree of
\kappa^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L}
and
\kappa^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{L}
and then deploy
Lemma

D.6
to characterize which eigenvalues of
k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L},k^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{L}
are strictly positive.

Lemma D.13
.

Let the activation function
\varphi
fulfill
5
and let
L\geq 2
.
We use the conventions
\infty^{0}\coloneqq 1
and
\infty-\infty\coloneqq-\infty
.
Then, the nonzero eigenvalues of the NNGP-kernel
k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L}
and the NTK-kernel
k^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{L}
defined in
D.7
fulfill

\displaystyle\mu_{2k+r,d}(k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L})

\displaystyle>0\text{ if and only if }\begin{cases}2k+r\leq\deg_{\text{ev}}\left({G_{L}}\right)&,r=0,\\
2k+r\leq\deg_{\text{od}}\left({G_{L}}\right)&,r=1,\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mu_{2k+r,d}(k^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{L})

\displaystyle>0\text{ if and only if }\begin{cases}2k+r\leq\deg_{\text{ev}}\left({H_{L}}\right)&,r=0,\\
2k+r\leq\deg_{\text{od}}\left({H_{L}}\right)&,r=1\penalty\ .\end{cases}

For the NNGP-term
G_{L}
define
\sigma^{2}\coloneqq\sigma_{i}^{2}\sigma_{b}^{2}
, and for the NTK-term
H_{L}
define
\sigma^{2}\coloneqq\sigma_{b}^{2}
. That is, the relevant row of the table is different for
G_{L}
or respectively for
H_{L}
in the case
\sigma_{i}^{2}=0,\ \sigma_{b}^{2}>0
.

The even/odd degree of
G_{L}
or respectively
H_{L}
is given by

\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(G_{L}\right),\deg_{\text{ev}}\left(H_{L}\right)

\deg_{\text{od}}\left(G_{L}\right),\deg_{\text{od}}\left(H_{L}\right)

\sigma^{2}>0
,
\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{ev}}\left(\varphi\right)>\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{od}}\left(\varphi\right)

\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{ev}}\left(\varphi\right)^{L-1}

\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{ev}}\left(\varphi\right)^{L-1}-1

\sigma^{2}>0
,
\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{ev}}\left(\varphi\right)<\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{od}}\left(\varphi\right)

\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{od}}\left(\varphi\right)^{L-1}-1

\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{od}}\left(\varphi\right)^{L-1}

\sigma^{2}=0,\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{ev}}\left(\varphi\right)>\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{od}}\left(\varphi\right)

\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{ev}}\left(\varphi\right)^{L-1}

\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{ev}}\left(\varphi\right)\left(\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{ev}}\left(\varphi\right)^{L-2}-1\right)+\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{od}}\left(\varphi\right)

\sigma^{2}=0,\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{ev}}\left(\varphi\right)<\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{od}}\left(\varphi\right)

\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{od}}\left(\varphi\right)\left(\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{od}}\left(\varphi\right)^{L-2}-1\right)+\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{ev}}\left(\varphi\right)

\deg^{\text{Her}}_{\text{od}}\left(\varphi\right)^{L-1}

Proof.

Let
\kappa:[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R},\kappa(t)=\sum_{j\geq 0}\lambda_{i}t^{j}
be a power series.
Let
k
be the corresponding radial kernel on
\mathbb{S}^{d}
given by
k(x,y)\coloneqq\kappa(\left\langle x,y\right\rangle)
and let
(\mu_{i})_{i\geq 0}
be the decreasing series of eigenvalues of
k
counted by algebraic multiplicity.

Reduction to polynomial degrees of
\kappa
:
We show that the largest even/odd index
i
corresponding to a nonzero eigenvalue
\mu_{i}\neq 0
of
k
is the same as the largest even/odd index
j
corresponding to a nonzero coefficient
\lambda_{j}
of the power series representation of
\kappa
. By convention, if there are infinitely many such indices, we say that the largest one equals
\infty
and we say that the largest even/odd nonzero index equals
-\infty
if all corresponding eigenvalues or respectively coefficients are equal to zero.

•

Hubbert et al. 2015
show that the eigenvalues
\mu_{i}

have the same sign as the Legendre coefficients
b_{k}(\kappa)
obtained by displaying
\kappa
by Legendre polynomials, which form an orthonormal basis of
L_{2}([-1,1])
.

•

The bases of the Legendre polynomials
(p_{i})_{i\geq 0}
, the Hermite polynomials
(h_{i})_{i\geq 0}
and the monomials
(t\mapsto t^{i})_{i\geq 0}
share the properties that the
i
-th element is a polynomial of degree
i
and is it even/odd if
i
is even/odd. Hence, when we can display
\kappa
as a sum in those bases, the largest even/odd index
i
of nonzero coefficients coincide.

•

Hence, we see that indeed the largest even/odd index of a nonzero eigenvalue of
k
corresponds to the largest even/odd index of a nonzero coefficient of the power series representation of
\kappa
.

Hence, in order to obtain the largest even/odd index of a nonzero eigenvalue of
k^{\mathrm{NNGP}}_{L}
or respectively
k^{\mathrm{NTK}}_{L}
it suffices to determine the largest even/odd index of the nonzero coefficients of the power series representation of
G_{L}
or respectively
H_{L}
.
Given a nonzero eigenvalue with even/odd index,
Lemma

D.6
shows that all eigenvalues with smaller even/odd index are strictly positive and we can conclude the claim.
Hence, all that remains is to show that
G_{L}
and
H_{L}
can be represented as a power series and that the even/odd degrees of
G_{l}
and
H_{l}
are as the table claims.

Simplifying Notation:
By
5
we have
\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{N}(0,1))
, enabling us to display
\varphi
in the Hermite basis as
\varphi(t)=\sum_{i\geq 0}\eta_{i}h_{i}(t)
.

A.2

shows

(D.25)

Essentially, we obtain for any

Appendix E
Polynomial boundedness of derivatives

Here, we prove
Proposition

6
. Specifically, we want to find a sufficient criterion for
C^{\infty}
-functions
f
such that all of their derivatives
f^{(k)}
are polynomially bounded. To this end, we leverage that typical activation functions use symbolic expressions whose derivatives can be expressed using the same set of symbols, e.g.
\tanh^{\prime}(x)=1-\tanh(x)^{2}
. Our goal is to find a set of
“base functions”
whose derivatives use the same base functions and which are polynomially bounded. We then exploit that the class of polynomially bounded functions is closed under addition, multiplication and composition and therefore also contains the derivatives of the
“base functions”
. Higher-order derivatives are then easily treated using induction. We first formally define the relevant function classes:

Definition E.1
.

Let
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
be an interval. For
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, let

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{(m)}(I)

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle\{f\in C^{m}(I)\mid\forall 0\leq k\leq m\penalty\ \exists a,b,q>0\penalty\ \forall x\in I:|f^{(k)}(x)|\leq a|x|^{q}+b\}\penalty\ .

We note that the class
\mathcal{S}^{(\infty)}(I)
from
4
satisfies
\mathcal{S}^{(\infty)}(I)=\bigcap_{m=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{S}^{(m)}(I)
.
∎

Now, we formally define some
“base functions”
:

Definition E.2
.

Define
\sigmoid,\softplus,\RBF:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle\sigmoid(x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle(1+e^{-x})^{-1}

\displaystyle\softplus(x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle\log(1+e^{-x})

\displaystyle\RBF(x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle e^{-x^{2}}\penalty\ .

Moreover, let
\Phi
be the CDF of the normal distribution
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.
∎

Lemma E.3
.

Let
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
be an interval and let
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\cup\{\infty\}
. Then,

1.

If
m\geq 1
, then
\mathcal{S}^{(m)}=\{f\in C^{1}(I)\mid f\in\mathcal{S}^{(0)}(I)\text{ and }f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{(m-1)}\}(I)
.

2.

\mathcal{S}^{(m)}(I)
is closed under addition, multiplication and composition of functions.

3.

\mathcal{S}^{(m)}(I)
contains all polynomials.

4.

\mathcal{S}^{(m)}(I)
contains
\sigmoid,\tanh,\softplus,\sin,\cos,\RBF
and
\Phi
.

Proof.

We prove the statements for
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, the case
m=\infty
easily follows.

1.

This statement is straightforward.

2.

For addition, the statement is trivial. Now, let
f,g\in\mathcal{S}^{(m)}
. Choose constants
C_{1},C_{2},q
such that
|f(x)|,|g(x)|\leq C_{1}+C_{2}|x|^{q}
. Then,
|f(x)g(x)|=O(|x|^{2q})
and
|f(g(x))|\leq C_{1}+C_{2}|g(x)|^{q}\leq C_{1}+C_{2}|C_{1}+C_{2}|x|^{q}|^{q}=O(|x|^{q^{2}})
. This shows
f\cdot g,f\circ g\in\mathcal{S}^{(0)}
. If
m=0
, we are done. Otherwise, we obtain

\displaystyle(f+g)^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f^{\prime}+g^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{(m-1)}t

\displaystyle(f\cdot g)^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f^{\prime}\cdot g+f\cdot g^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{(m-1)}

\displaystyle(f\circ g)^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(f^{\prime}\circ g)\cdot g^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{(m-1)}

since
\mathcal{S}^{(m-1)}
is closed under addition, multiplication and composition by the induction hypothesis. By (b), we have shown that
f\cdot g,f\circ g\in\mathcal{S}^{(m)}
.

3.

This is trivial.

4.

It is not hard to see that all of the mentioned functions are in
\mathcal{S}^{(0)}
. For the induction step, we assume that all of the mentioned functions are in
\mathcal{S}^{(m-1)}
. Since all of these functions are
C^{\infty}
functions, their derivatives are in
C^{m-1}(\mathbb{R})
. Moreover, by inspecting the derivatives and using the previously proven facts as well as the induction hypothesis, it is easy to see that the derivatives are in
\mathcal{S}^{(m-1)}(\mathbb{R})
:

\displaystyle\sigmoid^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigmoid(x)\cdot(1-\sigmoid(x))=\sigmoid(x)\cdot(x^{0}+(-x^{0})\cdot\sigmoid(x))

\displaystyle\tanh^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-\tanh(x)\cdot\tanh(x)

\displaystyle\softplus^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigmoid(x)

\displaystyle\sin^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\cos(x)

\displaystyle\cos^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sin(x)

\displaystyle\RBF^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2x\cdot\RBF(x)

\displaystyle\Phi^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-x^{2}/2}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\RBF(2^{-1/2}\cdot x)\penalty\ .

Hence, by (b), we have shown that the functions are in
\mathcal{S}^{(m)}
. ∎

See
6

Proof.

1.

It follows from elementary calculations that the functions
f(x)=\exp(ax)
are in
\mathcal{S}^{(\infty)}((-\infty,0])
for all
a\geq 0
. For the other functions, this follows from
Lemma

E.3
.

2.

This follows directly from (a). ∎

Appendix F
Smoothness of GP sample paths

To prove
Theorem

13
, we need the following result, which is a variant of Thm. 7.4 of
Lukić and Beder 2001
:

Theorem F.1

(Theorem 1.2 in
Steinwart 2024
)
.

Let
H_{1},H_{2}
be Hilbert spaces on a set
T
and let
X
be a centered Gaussian process with covariance kernel
k
such that
H_{1}
is the RKHS of
k
.
Then the following statements hold true:

1.

If
H_{1}\hookrightarrow H_{2}
and furthermore this embedding is a Hilbert-Schmidt operator, then there exists a version
Y
of
X
with
\mathbb{P}(Y\in H_{2}=1)
.

2.

Otherwise, for all versions
Y
of
X
we have
\mathbb{P}(Y\in H_{2}=0)
.

Now, we can prove the desired theorem:

See
13

Proof.

In case ii), the requirement
\varepsilon<\alpha
stems from the subsequently used theory, which requires the investigated spaces to be RKHSs, which is for Sobolev spaces
H^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{d})
equivalent to
s>d/2
.

Considering the Sobolev space
H^{\alpha+d/2+s}(\mathbb{S}^{d})
for
s\in\mathbb{R}
we observe that for
s>d/2
we have

H^{d+\alpha}(\mathbb{S}^{d})\not\hookrightarrow H^{d/2+\alpha+s}(\mathbb{S}^{d})
and hence we can apply
Theorem

F.1
b).
For
s\leq d/2
, the embedding
H^{d+\alpha}(\mathbb{S}^{d})\hookrightarrow H^{d/2+\alpha+s}(\mathbb{S}^{d})
exists and it remains to investigate when this embedding is a Hilbert-Schmidt operator. For
r\geq 0
,

Brauchart and Dick 2013
shows that the Sobolev space
H^{r}(\mathbb{S}^{d})
is given by

\displaystyle H^{r}(\mathbb{S}^{d})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{d})\mid\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{H^{r}(\mathbb{S}^{d})}<\infty\},

\displaystyle\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{H^{r}(\mathbb{S}^{d})}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{l,d}}\lambda_{l,r}\hbox{\hskip for a sequence

where furthermore
N_{l,d}=\Theta_{\forall l}((l+1)^{d-1})
holds.
Hence,
(\lambda_{l,r}^{-1/2}Y_{l,i})_{l\geq 0,1\leq i\leq N_{l,d}}
forms an ONB of
H^{r}(\mathbb{S}^{d})
which diagonalizes the embedding operator.
The Hilbert-Schmidt norm of the embedding
H^{d+\alpha}(\mathbb{S}^{d})\hookrightarrow H^{d/2+\alpha+r}(\mathbb{S}^{d})
is now straightforwardly given, cf.
Birman and Solomjak 1987
[Chapter 11.2], by

\displaystyle\left\lVert H^{d+\alpha}(\mathbb{S}^{d})\hookrightarrow H^{d/2+\alpha+r}(\mathbb{S}^{d})\right\rVert_{\text{HS}}=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{l,d}}\frac{\lambda_{l,d/2+\alpha+r}}{\lambda_{l,d+\alpha}}\penalty\ .

(F.1)

The asymptotics of
\lambda_{l,s}
and
N_{l,d}
yield

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N_{l,d}}\frac{\lambda_{l,d/2+\alpha+r}}{\lambda_{l,d+\alpha}}=\Theta_{\forall l}(l+1)^{d-1}\frac{\Theta_{\forall l}(l+1)^{d+2\alpha+r}}{\Theta_{\forall l}(l+1)^{2d+2\alpha}}=\Theta_{\forall l}(l+1)^{r-1}

and see that (
F.1
) is finite if and only if
r<0
, and the claim follows.∎
